Question 124

Which statement about Banker’s algorithm is false?

  • A. It is a CPU scheduling algorithm
  • B. It uses each process’s declared maximum resource claims
  • C. Its safety test simulates completions to check whether every process can finish
  • D. It checks a received resource request before granting it

Adapted from

  • Computer Science model exam collection · Question 32

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compute remaining needs and simulate feasible completions before deciding whether an allocation is safe.

Study-guide sections

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

Correct answer: A — It is a CPU scheduling algorithm

Banker’s algorithm is a deadlock-avoidance technique, not a CPU scheduling policy. It requires maximum resource claims, computes remaining needs, and simulates possible completions to check for a safe sequence. A request is evaluated after receipt and before being granted, including checks against need and availability. The factual alternatives therefore describe the supported safety procedure, leaving only the CPU-scheduling classification false.

Evidence in the complete study note

Avoidance allows requests only if the resulting state remains safe. Banker requires maximum claims. Compute Need=Max−Allocation. Set Work=Available and repeatedly find an unfinished process with Need≤Work componentwise; simulate completion by adding its Allocation to Work. Finishing all gives a safe sequence. Tentatively grant a request only if it is within Need and Available and the safety test succeeds. Unsafe does not mean already deadlocked. Banker is avoidance, not CPU scheduling, detection or prevention by breaking a condition.OS25

Choice by choice

A · correct — Banker is deadlock avoidance rather than CPU scheduling, so this statement is false.

B · incorrect — Maximum resource claims are required input, so this statement is true.

C · incorrect — The safety test repeatedly simulates feasible completions, so this statement is true.

D · incorrect — A request is checked before granting it, so this statement is true.

Question 125

A set of processes is blocked: each holds a resource and waits for another resource that only another blocked member of the set can release. No member can proceed without intervention. This state is called:

  • A. Deadlock
  • B. Queuing
  • C. Preemption
  • D. Overloading

Adapted from

  • Computer Science model exam collection · Question 45

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

Study-guide sections

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

Correct answer: A — Deadlock

The specified set has a closed dependency: every member is waiting for something only another blocked member can provide. None can reach the release operation needed by another, so the state is deadlock. Queuing can occur during ordinary waits that eventually finish; preemption withdraws or reallocates a resource; overload concerns excessive demand. The added dependency condition makes the deadlock interpretation definite.

Evidence in the complete study note

A set is deadlocked when each member waits for an event/resource that only another blocked member can provide. Mere waiting for another holder is normal blocking; indefinite unfair delay can be starvation without a cycle of dependencies.OS22
Prevention constrains how requests are made. Share a resource where safe to remove mutual exclusion; request all needed resources before holding any to remove hold-and-wait; permit safe resource withdrawal/restart to attack no-preemption; impose a global acquisition order to remove circular wait.OS24

Choice by choice

A · correct — The closed dependency among blocked members defines deadlock.

B · incorrect — Ordinary queuing does not require a dependency that prevents every member from progressing.

C · incorrect — Preemption is resource withdrawal or reassignment rather than this blocked state.

D · incorrect — Overload does not by itself create the specified closed dependency.

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Correct answer: D — Device drivers

A device driver translates OS I/O requests into the particular operations a device requires. The kernel provides broader resource management and can contain drivers, but the question asks for that specific hardware-facing component. D · correct — Drivers convert OS requests into device-specific operations.

Question 132

Which scheduling algorithm is based on assigning a fixed time slice to each process in a round-robin manner?

  • A. First-Come, First-Served (FCFS) scheduling
  • B. Shortest Job Next (SJN) scheduling
  • C. Priority scheduling
  • D. Round-Robin scheduling

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 659

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace bounded turns in ready-queue order and include the stated overhead and arrivals.

Study-guide sections

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

Correct answer: D — Round-Robin scheduling

PDF p25 explicitly defines a fixed execution quantum followed by preemption and another process’s turn. D is that round-robin policy.

Evidence in the complete study note

RR serves a ready queue cyclically for at most quantum q per turn. Unfinished jobs return to the tail; completed/blocked jobs leave.OS18
FCFS runs ready bursts in arrival order and ordinarily does not preempt.OS15
Non-preemptive SJF/SJN chooses the shortest next burst among currently ready jobs.OS16
Priority scheduling chooses the highest-priority ready job.OS17

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — FCFS uses arrival order without this specified repeating quantum (p23).

B · incorrect — SJN selects short jobs using known execution length (p23).

C · incorrect — Priority uses assigned rank (p24).

D · correct — Fixed quantum plus repeated turns is the p25 RR definition.

Question 133

What is the primary goal of file-access authorization in an operating system?

  • A. To ensure the availability of files for authorized users
  • B. To prevent unauthorized access to files and data
  • C. To optimize file storage and retrieval performance
  • D. To minimize the impact of system failures on file operations

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 660

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish identity checks, permission checks and the particular security property at issue.

Study-guide sections

OS39 · Protection, authentication and threats

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS39 · Protection, authentication and threats

Correct answer: B — To prevent unauthorized access to files and data

Authorization checks whether a user or other principal is permitted to perform an operation on a file. Preventing unauthorized access is therefore the direct goal. Availability and recovery protect access through failures or disruption, while storage optimization concerns performance. Those are useful objectives, but they are distinct from deciding who may read, write or otherwise access a particular file.

Evidence in the complete study note

Authentication verifies identity; authorization checks permitted operations. An ACL lists principals and rights; owner/group/other bits are a simpler policy model, not universal defaults granting all owners full access or everyone read. Read, write, execute, append and delete are different rights, with filesystem-specific rules.OS39

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Availability keeps files accessible when needed; it is distinct from deciding whether an attempted access is authorized.

B · correct — Preventing unauthorized access directly follows from checking permitted operations and file rights.

C · incorrect — Storage and retrieval performance do not determine a caller’s access rights.

D · incorrect — Limiting failure impact concerns resilience and recovery, rather than the access-permission decision.

Question 134

What is the purpose of virtual memory in an operating system?

  • A. To provide a virtualized environment for running multiple operating systems
  • B. To increase the physical memory capacity of a computer system
  • C. To allow the execution of programs without any memory constraints
  • D. To create a separate memory space for each running process

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 661

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS32 · Virtual memory and page-fault handling

OS04 · APIs, system calls and virtualization

Correct answer: D — Provide translated, protected per-process address spaces.

OS32 defines virtual memory as translated/protected address spaces with nonresident portions backed by storage. D is the option matching separate process memory spaces. A describes machine virtualization; B falsely adds physical RAM and C falsely removes all memory constraints. Isolation and translation are the supported purpose, with backing storage providing flexibility within finite resource limits.

Evidence in the complete study note

Virtual memory provides translated, protected address spaces and allows nonresident portions of a program to be backed by storage. It does not add physical RAM or remove all capacity constraints.OS32
Virtualization presents a virtual machine environment, often allowing guest operating systems on one host through a hypervisor. It differs from virtual memory, which translates a process’s addresses.OS04

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Running multiple guest OSs describes machine virtualization, explicitly distinguished from process virtual memory.

B · incorrect — Virtual memory does not physically add RAM; it maps address spaces using existing RAM and backing storage.

C · incorrect — The note explicitly retains capacity constraints; virtual memory does not make execution memory-unlimited.

D · correct — Separate translated/protected process address spaces match the virtual-memory purpose described here.

Question 136

What is the primary purpose of process synchronization when processes perform conflicting accesses to shared state?

  • A. To prevent deadlock situations
  • B. To ensure fair allocation of system resources
  • C. To enable parallel execution of multiple processes
  • D. To coordinate shared-state access and required operation ordering safely

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 663

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Identify conflicting shared accesses and the safety or progress property required.

Study-guide sections

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

Correct answer: D — To coordinate shared-state access and required operation ordering safely

Conflicting accesses need coordination so interleaving does not corrupt shared state or violate required operation order. Critical-section protection provides mutual exclusion, with progress and bounded waiting required under the stated solution assumptions. Synchronization does not automatically eliminate deadlock; inconsistent lock acquisition can introduce it. It may also serialize work rather than create parallelism, so safe shared-access coordination is the precise primary purpose.

Evidence in the complete study note

A race occurs when results depend on uncoordinated interleaving of conflicting accesses. A critical section is code accessing shared state that needs protection, not the resource itself. A correct exclusion solution requires mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting under stated assumptions. Mutual exclusion is a desired safety property; races, starvation and deadlock are possible failures.OS10
Keep critical regions small and ensure every conflicting access uses the same protection. A barrier waits until a defined set of participants reaches a point; a monitor encapsulates protected operations with condition synchronization. Locking can itself introduce deadlock if acquisition order is inconsistent.OS10

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Locking can itself deadlock, so universal deadlock prevention is not the primary definition.

B · incorrect — General resource-allocation fairness is broader than coordinating shared-state operations.

C · incorrect — Synchronization can serialize conflicting operations and does not itself create parallel execution.

D · correct — Coordinating shared accesses and required ordering safely captures the primary purpose.

Question 142

How does an operating system improve efficiency in terms of process management?

  • A. By providing a comprehensive programming environment
  • B. By executing processes in parallel on multiple CPUs
  • C. By automatically compressing files to reduce storage space
  • D. By enabling users to schedule program execution at specific times

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS02 · Batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing and real time

OS08 · Threads and mapping models

Correct answer: B — By executing processes in parallel on multiple CPUs

PDF p29 says the kernel can simultaneously schedule threads from one process across multiple processors, while p27 explains parallel performance benefits. B is the relevant offered mechanism when suitable parallel work and CPUs exist.

Evidence in the complete study note

A single CPU interleaves execution; multiple CPUs can execute work truly in parallel. Shared-memory multiprocessors do not require each CPU to have private main memory.OS02
In many-to-one mapping, a blocking kernel call can block all user threads and one kernel execution context cannot use multiple CPUs simultaneously. One-to-one permits another kernel thread to run when one blocks. Many-to-many multiplexes user threads over several kernel threads.OS08

In the context of operating systems, what is the meaning of scalability?

  • A. The ability to run on different hardware platforms
  • B. The ability to support multiple programming languages
  • C. The ability to handle increasing workloads and resources
  • D. The ability to provide a user-friendly interface

Separate broad resource-management goals from individual services or applications.

Study-guide sections

OS01 · Objectives, resource management and evolution

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS01 · Objectives, resource management and evolution

Correct answer: C — Handle increasing workloads and resources.

OS01 directly defines scalability as handling greater workloads/resources. Running on different hardware platforms is portability, and providing an easy interface is convenience. Programming-language support does not itself establish either increasing capacity or workload handling. The wording in C matches the supplied definition without assuming scalability guarantees limitless growth or unchanged performance.

Evidence in the complete study note

Scalability means handling greater workload/resources; portability means adapting to other platforms.OS01
Convenience means making tasks easy through consistent interfaces, files and device abstractions.OS01

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Adapting to other hardware platforms is portability, explicitly distinguished from scalability.

B · incorrect — Supporting programming languages does not define the ability to handle growing workload or resources.

C · correct — Handling increasing workloads and resources directly matches the note’s definition of scalability.

D · incorrect — A user-friendly interface concerns convenience rather than scaling capacity as demand grows.

OS01 · Objectives, resource management and evolution

Correct answer: A — It continues correct service despite the specified failures it is designed to tolerate

Fault tolerance means that correct service continues despite specified failures within the system’s fault model. Recovery restores service after a failure and may be useful without providing uninterrupted correct service. Multiple-user handling concerns concurrency, resource-intensive performance concerns efficiency, and sensitive-data protection concerns security. The revised continuation option therefore states the specific property rather than substituting quick recovery for fault tolerance.

Question 147

What is deadlock in the standard process/resource model?

  • A. A state in which each member of a blocked set waits for an event or resource that only another blocked member can provide
  • B. It is a state where a process is terminated abruptly.
  • C. It is a state where multiple processes are executing concurrently.
  • D. It is a state where the operating system crashes due to a software bug.

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 674

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

Study-guide sections

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Correct answer: A — A state in which each member of a blocked set waits for an event or resource that only another blocked member can provide

Deadlock describes a blocked set whose members depend on one another for the events or resources required to continue. Because each provider is itself blocked within the same dependency, normal execution cannot resolve the waits. A process being terminated, multiple processes executing, or an OS crash describes a different condition. The revised definition also distinguishes deadlock from starvation caused by unfair repeated bypass.

Evidence in the complete study note

A set is deadlocked when each member waits for an event/resource that only another blocked member can provide. Mere waiting for another holder is normal blocking; indefinite unfair delay can be starvation without a cycle of dependencies.OS22
New processes are created/admitted; ready means eligible for CPU but not currently running. Running uses a CPU. Waiting/blocked means unable to proceed until an event; terminated means execution has ended.OS06

Choice by choice

A · correct — This states the closed dependency that defines the deadlocked set.

B · incorrect — Abrupt termination ends execution rather than defining blocked dependencies.

C · incorrect — Concurrent execution is normal and does not imply deadlock.

D · incorrect — An OS crash is a different failure; deadlocked processes can exist while the OS remains active.

Question 148

Which of the following conditions must be present for deadlock to occur?

  • A. Mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption, and circular wait.
  • B. Mutual exclusion, hold and wait, preemption, and circular wait.
  • C. Mutual inclusion, hold and wait, no preemption, and circular wait.
  • D. Mutual inclusion, hold and wait, preemption, and circular wait.

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 675

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

Study-guide sections

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: A — Mutual exclusion, hold-and-wait, no-preemption and circular wait.

OS22 lists the four necessary conditions in the standard reusable-resource model. A preserves the exact set: exclusion, holding while requesting more, no forced resource withdrawal, and a wait cycle. B introduces preemption in place of no-preemption; C and D replace exclusion with inclusion. These are not harmless wording changes because they remove or reverse the relevant restrictions.

Evidence in the complete study note

Mutual exclusion allows at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. Hold-and-wait means retaining a resource while requesting another. No resource preemption means release must be voluntary. Circular wait forms a closed chain of such waits.OS22
All four are necessary in the standard reusable-resource model. Their availability as general system policies does not prove a deadlock exists at this instant; inspect actual allocations/requests.OS22

Choice by choice

A · correct — This option states all four standard necessary conditions with their correct meanings and polarity.

B · incorrect — It substitutes preemption for no-preemption, reversing the required resource-withdrawal restriction.

C · incorrect — It substitutes mutual inclusion for mutual exclusion, losing the nonshareable-resource condition.

D · incorrect — It reverses both exclusion and no-preemption, so it is not the required condition set.

Question 149

What is mutual exclusion in the context of deadlock?

  • A. It is the condition where multiple processes can access the same resource simultaneously.
  • B. It is the condition where a resource can be accessed by only one process at a time.
  • C. It is the condition where a process holds a resource while waiting for another resource.

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: B — At most one process holds the nonshareable resource at a time.

OS22 defines mutual exclusion as allowing at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. B states that one-at-a-time condition. A describes concurrent sharing, C describes holding while requesting another resource, and D describes indefinite waiting without establishing exclusivity. The definition concerns access safety, not a guarantee that every waiting process eventually obtains the resource.

Question 150

What is hold and wait in the context of deadlock?

  • A. It is the condition where a process holds a resource while waiting for another resource.
  • B. It is the condition where a process waits indefinitely for a resource.
  • C. It is the condition where multiple processes can access the same resource simultaneously.

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: A — Retain a resource while waiting for another.

OS22 directly defines hold-and-wait as retaining a resource while requesting another. A contains both parts of that definition. Merely waiting indefinitely does not establish a retained allocation, simultaneous sharing contradicts exclusion, and one-at-a-time access describes mutual exclusion rather than a process holding one resource while requesting more.

Evidence in the complete study note

Mere waiting for another holder is normal blocking; indefinite unfair delay can be starvation without a cycle of dependencies. Mutual exclusion allows at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. Question 151

What is preemption in the context of deadlock prevention?

  • A. It is the condition where a process holds a resource while waiting for another resource.
  • B. It is the condition where a process waits indefinitely for a resource.
  • C. It is the condition where multiple processes can access the same resource simultaneously.
  • D. It is the condition where a resource can be forcibly taken from a process.

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 678

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Identify the resource-request restriction and which necessary condition it removes.

Study-guide sections

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: D — A resource may be forcibly taken from its holder.

OS22 defines no-preemption as voluntary release only, while OS24 permits safe withdrawal/restart as a way to attack that restriction. Preemption is the opposite permission: a resource can be forcibly reclaimed. D directly states it. The answer concerns resources that can safely be withdrawn, not a claim that every device or lock can be preempted without corruption.

Evidence in the complete study note

Prevention constrains how requests are made. Share a resource where safe to remove mutual exclusion; request all needed resources before holding any to remove hold-and-wait; permit safe resource withdrawal/restart to attack no-preemption; impose a global acquisition order to remove circular wait. Not every physical resource is shareable or safely preemptable.OS24
Mere waiting for another holder is normal blocking; indefinite unfair delay can be starvation without a cycle of dependencies. Mutual exclusion allows at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. Hold-and-wait means retaining a resource while requesting another. No resource preemption means release must be voluntary. Circular wait forms a closed chain of such waits.OS22

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Holding one resource while waiting for another describes hold-and-wait rather than forced resource withdrawal.

B · incorrect — Indefinite waiting is a possible outcome, not the permission to reclaim a held resource.

C · incorrect — Concurrent sharing concerns resource shareability rather than withdrawing an existing allocation from its holder.

D · correct — Forcibly taking a held resource is resource preemption, subject to safe withdrawal/restart support.

Question 152

What is circular wait in the context of deadlock?

  • A. It is the condition where a process waits indefinitely for a resource.
  • B. It is the condition where multiple processes can access the same resource simultaneously.
  • C. It is the condition where a process holds a resource while waiting for another resource.
  • D. It is the condition where a circular chain of processes exists, each holding a resource requested by the next process in the chain.

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 679

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

Study-guide sections

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: D — A closed chain of resource waits.

OS22 defines circular wait as a closed chain of waits. D specifies that each process holds a resource requested by the next in a circular chain, which returns the dependency to the starting participant. C describes only one hold-and-wait relationship, which need not be cyclic. Indefinite waiting and simultaneous sharing likewise fail to establish a closed chain.

Evidence in the complete study note

Mere waiting for another holder is normal blocking; indefinite unfair delay can be starvation without a cycle of dependencies. Mutual exclusion allows at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. Hold-and-wait means retaining a resource while requesting another. No resource preemption means release must be voluntary. Circular wait forms a closed chain of such waits.OS22

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Indefinite waiting does not identify a closed cycle of dependencies among resource holders.

B · incorrect — Simultaneous resource sharing is not a chain of blocked requests and does not define circular wait.

C · incorrect — One process holding while requesting another establishes hold-and-wait but not necessarily a cycle.

D · correct — The circular chain of holdings and requests explicitly forms the required closed wait dependency.

Question 153

Which listed policy does not actively prevent an unsafe allocation or detect and recover from an existing deadlock? Interpret “deadlock acceptance” as deliberately ignoring sufficiently rare deadlocks.

  • A. Deadlock avoidance
  • B. Deadlock detection and recovery
  • C. Deadlock prevention
  • D. Deadlock acceptance

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS26 · Detection, recovery and intentional ignoring

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

Correct answer: D — Deadlock acceptance

Prevention constrains requests to break a necessary condition; avoidance keeps tentative allocations safe; detection and recovery identifies and resolves an existing deadlock. Deliberate acceptance, as defined here, instead ignores a sufficiently rare problem rather than actively applying those controls. It is still a possible policy choice, but is the only listed one outside the active-control category specified by the revised stem.

Evidence in the complete study note

Detection asks whether the current state is deadlocked. Recovery can terminate selected processes or reclaim safely preemptable resources and roll affected work back to a checkpoint. Termination and rollback are both possible recovery tools; choose according to the resource and process conditions.OS26
Ignoring a sufficiently rare problem is sometimes called the ostrich policy, but real systems still prevent or detect selected deadlocks.OS26
Prevention constrains how requests are made.OS24
Avoidance allows requests only if the resulting state remains safe.OS25

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Avoidance actively restricts grants to preserve a safe state.

B · incorrect — Detection and recovery actively identifies and resolves deadlock.

C · incorrect — Prevention actively removes a necessary deadlock condition.

D · correct — Intentional ignoring is a policy choice but does not actively apply the specified controls.

Question 154

In a resource-allocation graph with one instance of each resource type, which listed roles are valid? Read B as direct resource-to-resource edges and D as showing permission to forcibly withdraw resources. Select all that apply.

  • A. To represent the allocation of resources to processes.
  • B. To represent the dependencies between resources.
  • C. To represent the circular wait condition.
  • D. To represent the preemption of resources.

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 681

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Follow request and assignment arrows and check how many instances each resource has.

Study-guide sections

OS23 · Resource graphs and missing-diagram limits

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS23 · Resource graphs and missing-diagram limits

Correct answers (select all): A, C — To represent the allocation of resources to processes.; To represent the circular wait condition.

An assignment edge records which process holds a resource instance, and a request edge records what a process is waiting for. Following alternating edges can form a circular wait. In the stated single-instance model a cycle establishes deadlock. The graph does not use direct resource-to-resource dependency edges and does not encode a general permission to preempt resources, so A and C are the valid listed roles.

Evidence in the complete study note

In a resource-allocation graph, P→R is a request and an instance R→P is an assignment. Follow these directions to form waits. No cycle implies no deadlock; with one instance of every resource type, a cycle implies deadlock. With multiple instances a cycle alone is insufficient: another instance may permit completion. A wait-for graph replaces single-instance resource nodes with P→Q when P waits for Q.OS23

Choice by choice

A · correct — Assignment edges represent resource holdings by processes.

B · incorrect — Standard graph edges connect processes and resources, not resources directly to other resources.

C · correct — A cycle of request and assignment edges represents circular wait.

D · incorrect — Resource-preemption permission is not encoded by the stated request/assignment edges.

Question 155

Which resource allocation policy can help prevent deadlock?

  • A. First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)
  • B. Round Robin (RR)
  • C. Shortest Job Next (SJN)
  • D. Banker's Algorithm

Source

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 682

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

Correct answer: D — Banker’s algorithm, technically an avoidance policy.

OS25 states that Banker grants requests only while preserving a safe state. That can prevent entry into a deadlocked state in the broad everyday sense used by can help prevent, although its technical category is avoidance rather than condition-breaking prevention. FCFS, RR and SJN are CPU scheduling policies and do not themselves test resource-allocation safety.

Evidence in the complete study note

Avoidance allows requests only if the resulting state remains safe. Banker requires maximum claims. Compute Need=Max−Allocation.OS25
Non-preemptive scheduling keeps a running CPU burst until completion, blocking or voluntary yielding. Preemptive scheduling can return running work to ready, usually after a timer quantum or a more urgent arrival. FCFS and non-preemptive SJF are non-preemptive; RR and SRTF are preemptive. Priority can be either, so its name alone cannot decide. Cooperative describes relying on voluntary release.OS14

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — FCFS orders CPU bursts by arrival rather than testing maximum resource claims and safe allocations.

B · incorrect — RR schedules CPU turns; time slicing does not establish safe allocation of locks or other resources.

C · incorrect — SJN selects shortest next CPU bursts rather than performing a resource-state safety test.

D · correct — Banker admits resource requests only when the resulting state is safe; its strict classification is avoidance.

Question 156

What is the purpose of the Banker's Algorithm in deadlock avoidance?

  • A. To break the hold and wait condition.
  • B. To allocate resources in a fair and equitable manner.
  • C. To detect deadlocked processes and recover from deadlock.
  • D. To ensure that resource requests will not lead to an unsafe state.

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 683

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compute remaining needs and simulate feasible completions before deciding whether an allocation is safe.

Study-guide sections

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

OS26 · Detection, recovery and intentional ignoring

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

OS26 · Detection, recovery and intentional ignoring

Correct answer: D — Ensure each granted request preserves a safe state.

Banker’s algorithm checks whether a tentative resource grant preserves a safe state. It computes remaining needs and simulates an order in which all processes can finish, granting only requests within the declared need and available resources that pass this safety test. It is an avoidance policy, not a fairness guarantee, an already-deadlocked-state detector, or prevention by eliminating hold-and-wait.

Evidence in the complete study note

Avoidance allows requests only if the resulting state remains safe. Banker requires maximum claims. Compute Need=Max−Allocation. Set Work=Available and repeatedly find an unfinished process with Need≤Work componentwise; simulate completion by adding its Allocation to Work. Finishing all gives a safe sequence. Tentatively grant a request only if it is within Need and Available and the safety test succeeds. Unsafe does not mean already deadlocked. Banker is avoidance, not CPU scheduling, detection or prevention by breaking a condition.OS25
Prevention constrains how requests are made.OS24
Detection asks whether the current state is deadlocked. Recovery can terminate selected processes or reclaim safely preemptable resources and roll affected work back to a checkpoint.OS26

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Banker does not require removing hold-and-wait; it checks whether tentative allocation remains safe.

B · incorrect — Its safety criterion is not a guarantee of fair or equal allocation among processes.

C · incorrect — Detection and recovery address actual deadlock, whereas Banker checks safety before granting a request.

D · correct — Preserving a safe state after each grant is the purpose of the Banker avoidance test.

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

Correct answers (select all): B, C — First-Come, First-Served (FCFS); Shortest Job Next (SJN)

FCFS ordinarily serves each ready burst in arrival order without forcibly taking the CPU away. SJN, as explicitly defined, chooses the shortest ready burst and then lets it continue non-preemptively. RR can interrupt a burst at its quantum boundary, while the stated priority variant interrupts for a higher-priority arrival. Thus FCFS and SJN are the two non-preemptive policies in this list.

Evidence in the complete study note

Non-preemptive scheduling keeps a running CPU burst until completion, blocking or voluntary yielding. Preemptive scheduling can return running work to ready, usually after a timer quantum or a more urgent arrival. FCFS and non-preemptive SJF are non-preemptive; RR and SRTF are preemptive. Priority can be either, so its name alone cannot decide. Cooperative describes relying on voluntary release.OS14
Priority scheduling chooses the highest-priority ready job. Check whether a smaller or larger numeric value means higher priority. State whether a high-priority arrival preempts.OS17

B · correct — FCFS ordinarily lets the selected burst continue without forced preemption.

C · correct — SJN is explicitly the non-preemptive shortest-next-burst policy.

D · incorrect — This option explicitly preempts for a higher-priority arrival.

OS20 · Fair-share, proportional shares and guarantees

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS20 · Fair-share, proportional shares and guarantees

Correct answer: D — Fair-Share Scheduling

Equal-turn RR gives each of the five tasks roughly one fifth of CPU service, so user A receives about 80 percent and B about 20 percent. Equal per-user fair-share instead gives each user half, dividing A’s half among its four tasks. FCFS and SJN do not enforce that user entitlement. The explicitly defined participant scope makes fair-share the unique matching policy.

Evidence in the complete study note

Fair-share scheduling allocates according to stated weights, potentially per user/group rather than per process. Equal per-process RR can let a user obtain more aggregate CPU by launching more processes. Proportional shares address that policy choice; fair need not mean equal.OS20
If user A runs four ready tasks and B runs one, plain RR gives A about 80% overall. A policy granting each user half the CPU instead gives A’s tasks about 12.5% each and B’s task 50%.OS20

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Per-task RR gives A four fifths of service rather than equal per-user shares.

B · incorrect — FCFS orders bursts by arrival and does not enforce per-user entitlements.

C · incorrect — SJN favors short bursts and does not allocate equal user shares.

D · correct — Equal per-user fair-share weights directly implement the stated half-and-half goal.

Question 161

A ready target has finitely many jobs ahead, all CPU bursts and RR quanta are finite and positive, context overhead is finite, and later arrivals join the tail without bypassing existing peers. Infinitely many later short or high-priority jobs may arrive over time. Which policies prevent that target from being postponed forever by later arrivals? D uses fixed priorities without aging. Select all that apply.

  • A. Round Robin
  • B. First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)
  • C. Shortest Job Next (SJN)
  • D. Fixed-priority scheduling without aging

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 688

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Follow arrival order, include idle periods and distinguish waiting from completion time.

Study-guide sections

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

Correct answers (select all): A, B — Round Robin; First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)

RR gives the target a turn after the finite work ahead has received bounded turns. FCFS serves the finite earlier bursts before later arrivals, so the target eventually starts. SJN can repeatedly prefer later short jobs, and fixed priority can repeatedly prefer later high-priority jobs. The assumptions distinguish guaranteed eventual service from policies that permit an endless stream to bypass the target.

Evidence in the complete study note

FCFS runs ready bursts in arrival order and ordinarily does not preempt. If the CPU is idle, jump to the next arrival. Long CPU-bound work can make many short jobs wait: the convoy effect. A finite burst with finitely many jobs ahead eventually starts, but waiting can still be large.OS15
RR serves a ready queue cyclically for at most quantum q per turn. Unfinished jobs return to the tail; completed/blocked jobs leave.OS18
Fixed-priority scheduling can starve low-priority jobs under continued high-priority load. SJF can also starve long jobs; starvation is not exclusive to fixed-priority scheduling.OS17

Choice by choice

A · correct — RR’s finite cyclic turns reach the target without later-arrival bypass.

B · correct — FCFS drains the finite predecessor work before serving later arrivals.

C · incorrect — An endless sequence of short arrivals can keep a long target waiting under SJN.

D · incorrect — An endless sequence of higher-priority arrivals can starve a low-priority target without aging.

Question 162

Which scheduling algorithm is based on the estimated remaining time required for each task?

  • A. Round Robin
  • B. First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)
  • C. Shortest Job Next (SJN)
  • D. Shortest Remaining Time First (SRTF)

Compare the ready jobs using the stated burst or remaining-time rule.

Study-guide sections

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

Correct answer: D — Shortest Remaining Time First (SRTF).

OS16 says SRTF reconsiders on arrivals and selects the shortest remaining work. This matches the estimated remaining-time criterion in the stem. SJN chooses the shortest next burst non-preemptively, while RR uses cyclic quanta and FCFS arrival order. The distinction matters after execution has begun: the originally shortest burst need not be the currently shortest remaining job.

Evidence in the complete study note

Non-preemptive SJF/SJN chooses the shortest next burst among currently ready jobs. SRTF reconsiders on arrivals and selects the shortest remaining work; a shorter newcomer can preempt. Future burst lengths normally need estimates.OS16
Repeated short arrivals can starve a long job. The shortest total original burst is not necessarily the shortest remaining burst after execution has begun.OS16

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — RR rotates bounded turns rather than selecting the job with the smallest estimated remaining execution.

B · incorrect — FCFS follows arrival order rather than comparing estimated remaining CPU work.

C · incorrect — Non-preemptive SJN compares next burst lengths when selecting, not continuously reconsidered remaining work.

D · correct — SRTF explicitly selects the shortest remaining work and can preempt on a shorter arrival.

Question 165

For a fixed finite set of simultaneous ready jobs with known CPU bursts, no I/O and zero switch cost, which listed non-preemptive policy minimizes average waiting over all possible execution orders for arbitrary burst lengths?

  • A. Round Robin
  • B. First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)
  • C. Shortest Job Next (SJN)
  • D. Shortest Remaining Time First (SRTF)

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 692

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare the ready jobs using the stated burst or remaining-time rule.

Study-guide sections

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

Correct answer: C — Shortest Job Next (SJN)

For the simultaneous known-burst workload, shortest-job order minimizes total waiting. Consider adjacent bursts L>S after an elapsed prefix T. Long then short gives waits T and T+L; short then long gives T and T+S, reducing their sum by L−S. Jobs outside the pair are unaffected. Repeatedly removing inversions gives SJN order. FCFS does not enforce it, while RR and SRTF are preemptive policies.

Evidence in the complete study note

Non-preemptive SJF/SJN chooses the shortest next burst among currently ready jobs. SRTF reconsiders on arrivals and selects the shortest remaining work; a shorter newcomer can preempt. Future burst lengths normally need estimates. SJF minimizes average waiting for the usual fixed simultaneous ready set with known bursts and no preemption/switch cost; that theorem is not a workload-independent comparison against every preemptive policy.OS16
For arrival A, first start S and completion C, response=S−A and turnaround=C−A. For a single CPU burst B with no I/O, waiting=C−A−B.OS13
FCFS and non-preemptive SJF are non-preemptive; RR and SRTF are preemptive.OS14

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — RR is preemptive and is outside the requested non-preemptive policy class.

B · incorrect — FCFS can tie on favorable orders but does not minimize waiting for arbitrary burst orders.

C · correct — SJN selects the shortest ready bursts and gives the standard minimum-average-waiting order.

D · incorrect — SRTF is the preemptive remaining-time policy rather than the requested non-preemptive one.

Question 167

Which listed policy is preemptive? SJN denotes non-preemptive shortest-job-next, and D specifically keeps the running burst until completion, blocking or voluntary release.

  • A. First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)
  • B. Round Robin
  • C. Shortest Job Next (SJN)
  • D. Non-preemptive Priority Scheduling

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 694

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check whether the running burst can be interrupted before completion or blocking.

Study-guide sections

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

Correct answer: B — Round Robin

RR limits a turn to a quantum and can return an unfinished runnable job to the ready queue. That is preemption. FCFS and the stated SJN variant keep a selected burst until it ends or blocks. The priority option is explicitly non-preemptive, so a higher-priority arrival does not interrupt its current burst. RR is therefore the unique preemptive policy in this list.

Evidence in the complete study note

Non-preemptive scheduling keeps a running CPU burst until completion, blocking or voluntary yielding. Preemptive scheduling can return running work to ready, usually after a timer quantum or a more urgent arrival. FCFS and non-preemptive SJF are non-preemptive; RR and SRTF are preemptive. Priority can be either, so its name alone cannot decide. Cooperative describes relying on voluntary release.OS14

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — FCFS ordinarily does not forcibly interrupt the selected burst.

B · correct — RR preempts unfinished work at quantum expiry.

C · incorrect — SJN is explicitly the non-preemptive shortest-job policy.

D · incorrect — The stated priority variant is explicitly non-preemptive.

Question 174

Three jobs all arrive at time 0 in listed order P1, P2, P3, with CPU bursts 24, 3, 3 and priorities 2, 1, 3 (smaller number is higher). There is no I/O or overhead. Compare FCFS, RR with quantum 4, non-preemptive SJN, and non-preemptive priority; use listed order for ties. Which has the highest average turnaround for this workload?

  • A. First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)
  • B. Round Robin
  • C. Shortest Job Next (SJN)
  • D. Priority Scheduling

Adapted from

  • Computer Science MCQ practice collection · Question 703

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Construct a timeline and distinguish completion rates from individual elapsed-time measures.

Study-guide sections

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

Correct answer: A — First-Come, First-Served (FCFS)

FCFS completes P1, P2, P3 at 24, 27 and 30, giving average turnaround 27. RR completes them at 30, 7 and 10, averaging 47/3. SJN completes short jobs at 3 and 6, then P1 at 30, averaging 13. Priority runs P2, P1, P3 and averages 20. FCFS is uniquely highest for this specified workload, without a universal worst-policy claim.

Evidence in the complete study note

For arrival A, first start S and completion C, response=S−A and turnaround=C−A. For a single CPU burst B with no I/O, waiting=C−A−B.OS13

B · incorrect — RR average turnaround is (30+7+10)/3=47/3, below 27.

C · incorrect — SJN average turnaround is (3+6+30)/3=13.

D · incorrect — Priority average turnaround is (3+27+30)/3=20.

Question 175

Which one of the following is a voluntary termination of a process?

  • A. Error exit
  • B. Killed by another process
  • C. Terminated by another process
  • D. Fatal exit

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 218

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Separate creation, image replacement and termination; classify termination by its cause.

Study-guide sections

OS07 · Creation, fork/exec and termination

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS07 · Creation, fork/exec and termination

Correct answer: A — Error exit, when the program detects an error and chooses to terminate.

OS07 explicitly classifies program-detected error exits as voluntary. Being killed or terminated by another process is imposed, and a fatal unrecoverable fault is involuntary. The distinction is the termination decision’s origin, not whether the run succeeded: a program can voluntarily exit because its input is invalid or it detects another handled error.

Evidence in the complete study note

Normal completion and a program-detected error exit are voluntary; an unrecoverable fault or being killed is involuntary. exit() terminates normally through the runtime; abort() requests abnormal termination.OS07

Choice by choice

A · correct — A program-detected error exit is a deliberate voluntary termination path, even though execution was unsuccessful.

B · incorrect — Being killed by another process is imposed externally and is therefore involuntary.

C · incorrect — Termination by another process is external rather than a voluntary exit chosen by the program.

D · incorrect — A fatal unrecoverable fault causes involuntary termination rather than a deliberate program error exit.

Question 176

Which statements are false in a system with one-to-one kernel-supported threads? In A, “children” means a process may create another process and a running thread may invoke a thread-creation operation; it does not require an OS thread-parent hierarchy. In B, “execute sequentially” means threads are required to finish one after another without interleaving. Select all that apply.

  • A. Both can create children
  • B. Threads within a process execute sequentially
  • C. Processes are not independent of one another
  • D. If one thread is blocked, another thread can run

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 219

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare per-thread execution context with shared process resources and the stated thread mapping.

Study-guide sections

OS08 · Threads and mapping models

OS07 · Creation, fork/exec and termination

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS08 · Threads and mapping models

OS07 · Creation, fork/exec and termination

Correct answers (select all): B, C — Threads within a process execute sequentially; Processes are not independent of one another

Threads within one process need not finish sequentially; their execution may interleave or run on multiple CPUs. Independent processes can also exist, so C’s universal dependence claim is false. The defined creation operations make A true without imposing a thread-parent hierarchy. Under one-to-one mapping, one blocked thread need not block every other thread, making D a valid possibility rather than a false statement.

Evidence in the complete study note

A thread has its own PC, registers and stack. Threads in a process share code, address space and usually open-file resources. A process can contain many threads; a thread belongs to one process at a time. Threads are not a group of processes and need not execute sequentially. Independent processes can exist; cooperating ones communicate/share data.OS08
One-to-one permits another kernel thread to run when one blocks.OS08
Processes arise during initialization, user requests, service requests or a running process’s creation call.OS07

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The defined process and thread creation operations are allowed, so A is true.

B · correct — Mandatory finish-before-next sequential execution is false; threads can interleave or run in parallel.

C · correct — Universal process dependence is false because independent processes can exist.

D · incorrect — One-to-one mapping permits another runnable thread to execute when one blocks, so D is true.

Question 177

Which statements are false under a classification based on simultaneous users? A single user may run multiple applications, and B means concurrent database accesses by those applications, not by additional users. In C, “at most one” describes the system’s supported capacity. Select all that apply.

  • A. In single user systems many users can login simultaneously
  • B. Single user systems can allow concurrent access to the database
  • C. Multiuser system can allow at most one user at a time
  • D. In Multiuser system many users can access the system concurrently

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 220

Compare interaction, CPU sharing and deadline requirements under the stated system model.

Study-guide sections

OS02 · Batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing and real time

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS02 · Batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing and real time

Correct answers (select all): A, C — In single user systems many users can login simultaneously; Multiuser system can allow at most one user at a time

Single-user and multiuser describe concurrent user access rather than application count. A therefore contradicts single-user status, and C contradicts the supported multiple-user capacity. One user may still run several applications accessing a database concurrently, which is the explicit meaning of B here. D states the multiuser capability correctly. The selected false statements are A and C.

Evidence in the complete study note

Single-user and multiuser describe concurrent user access, not the number of applications.OS02

Choice by choice

A · correct — Many simultaneous logged-in users contradict the single-user definition.

B · incorrect — Concurrent applications belonging to one user do not violate single-user status, so B is true.

C · correct — A capacity of at most one user contradicts the multiuser definition.

D · incorrect — Supporting concurrent access by many users is the multiuser capability, so D is true.

Question 178

When a process executes code that possibly changes the value of shared data, its execution has to be mutually exclusive. Therefore, the process is in:

  • A. Deadlock
  • B. Terminated
  • C. Critical section
  • D. Blocked

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam practice collection · Question 221

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Identify conflicting shared accesses and the safety or progress property required.

Study-guide sections

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: C — Critical section.

OS10 defines a critical section as code accessing shared state that needs protection. The stem describes execution that may change shared data and therefore requires mutual exclusion, directly matching that definition. Deadlock and blocking are waiting states or failures, while termination means execution has ended. None identifies the protected shared-state code currently being executed.

Evidence in the complete study note

A race occurs when results depend on uncoordinated interleaving of conflicting accesses. A critical section is code accessing shared state that needs protection, not the resource itself. A correct exclusion solution requires mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting under stated assumptions.OS10
New processes are created/admitted; ready means eligible for CPU but not currently running. Running uses a CPU. Waiting/blocked means unable to proceed until an event; terminated means execution has ended.OS06
A set is deadlocked when each member waits for an event/resource that only another blocked member can provide.OS22

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Deadlock involves blocked dependencies, whereas the stem describes protected code actively accessing shared data.

B · incorrect — A terminated process has ended execution, contradicting the stem’s currently executing shared-data operation.

C · correct — Code that accesses shared data and requires mutual exclusion is a critical section.

D · incorrect — A blocked process cannot proceed pending an event; the stem instead identifies the code requiring protection.

Question 179

Which one of the following function of operating system is categorized under process management?

  • A. Allocates the device in the efficient way
  • B. De-allocates memory when a process releases it.
  • C. Allocates the memory when the process requests it to do so.
  • D. Allocates the processor (CPU) to a process

Adapted from

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 29

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Decide whether the process is eligible, running or waiting, then identify the responsible queue or scheduling role.

Study-guide sections

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

OS-S03 · Memory allocation and page-policy decisions

OS01 · Objectives, resource management and evolution

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

OS-S03 · Memory allocation and page-policy decisions

OS01 · Objectives, resource management and evolution

Correct answer: D — Allocate the CPU to a process.

Allocating the CPU to a ready process is the scheduling role within process management. Device allocation belongs to device management. Allocating requested memory and reclaiming memory that has been released belong to memory management. Specifying the resource in the deallocation option removes its former overlap with several managers and leaves processor allocation as the distinct process-management function.

Evidence in the complete study note

Long-term scheduling admits jobs; medium-term scheduling suspends/swaps them; short-term scheduling selects ready work. The dispatcher performs the handoff/context restore after selection.OS06
Memory management tracks which primary-memory regions are free or in use, assigns memory to processes and data, and deallocates it when no longer needed or when a process terminates. Allocation and deallocation concern memory ownership; CPU selection belongs to scheduling.OS-S03
An OS allocates CPU time, memory, storage, devices and communication resources, and provides services to programs.OS01

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Allocating a device is a device-management responsibility.

B · incorrect — Reclaiming released memory is a memory-management responsibility under the stated resource classification.

C · incorrect — Allocating requested memory belongs to memory management.

D · correct — Selecting a ready process to receive the CPU is the process-scheduling responsibility.

Question 180

What is the primary function of an operating system?

  • C. To design computer hardware

OS01 · Objectives, resource management and evolution

Correct answer: D — Act as the service/resource intermediary between users/programs and hardware.

OS01 describes the OS allocating hardware resources and providing program services, with convenient interfaces and abstractions. D captures that intermediary role. Internet connectivity and document creation are narrower tasks, and hardware design is not a runtime OS service. The answer combines convenience and resource control rather than reducing the OS to one application or device function.

Evidence in the complete study note

An OS allocates CPU time, memory, storage, devices and communication resources, and provides services to programs. Convenience means making tasks easy through consistent interfaces, files and device abstractions. Efficiency means using resources effectively; it is not synonymous with the fastest response for every process. Application development tools use OS services but application development itself is not a core resource-management function.OS01

C · incorrect — Designing hardware is not the runtime resource/service function described for an operating system.

D · correct — The OS provides interfaces and controlled resource services linking users/programs to the underlying hardware.

Question 182

In a system, resources cannot be forcibly taken from processes; they must be released voluntarily. Which deadlock condition does this represent?

  • A. No Preemption
  • B. Hold and Wait
  • C. Mutual Exclusion
  • D. Circular Wait

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 57

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

Study-guide sections

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: A — No preemption.

OS22 defines no resource preemption as voluntary release by the current holder. The stem repeats that resources cannot be forcibly taken, giving a direct match. Holding while requesting another resource, one-at-a-time ownership and a closed chain of waits are separate conditions and do not by themselves express the withdrawal restriction.

Evidence in the complete study note

Mutual exclusion allows at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. Hold-and-wait means retaining a resource while requesting another. No resource preemption means release must be voluntary. Circular wait forms a closed chain of such waits.OS22

Choice by choice

A · correct — The holder must release voluntarily, so the system cannot forcibly preempt the resource.

B · incorrect — Hold-and-wait concerns retaining one resource while requesting another rather than whether withdrawal is allowed.

C · incorrect — Mutual exclusion limits simultaneous ownership, not whether allocated resources can be forcibly reclaimed.

D · incorrect — Circular wait concerns a closed dependency chain, not the resource-release rule specified here.

Question 183

How does an operating system improve efficiency in a multi-user environment?

  • A. By allowing only one user to access the system at a time
  • B. By disabling all background services
  • C. By using time-sharing and process scheduling to allocate CPU time fairly among users and processes
  • D. By requiring manual intervention for each task

Source

  • Computer Science Exit Exam · 2018 · Question 97

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare interaction, CPU sharing and deadline requirements under the stated system model.

Study-guide sections

OS02 · Batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing and real time

OS20 · Fair-share, proportional shares and guarantees

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS02 · Batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing and real time

OS20 · Fair-share, proportional shares and guarantees

Correct answer: C — Time-sharing and scheduling distribute CPU service among users/processes.

OS02 describes time-sharing as rapid scheduling/preemption supporting interactive users. OS20 explains that fair allocation needs a share policy and may operate per user/group. C describes those relevant mechanisms rather than disabling services or requiring manual intervention. Its fairly should be understood under a specified policy, not as an automatic equality guarantee from time-sharing alone.

Evidence in the complete study note

Multiprogramming keeps several jobs available so another can use the CPU while one waits; it does not require fixed time slices. Time-sharing uses rapid scheduling/preemption to support interactive users. A single CPU interleaves execution; multiple CPUs can execute work truly in parallel.OS02
Fair-share scheduling allocates according to stated weights, potentially per user/group rather than per process. Equal per-process RR can let a user obtain more aggregate CPU by launching more processes. Proportional shares address that policy choice; fair need not mean equal.OS20

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Restricting access to one user contradicts the multiuser setting rather than explaining its efficient sharing.

B · incorrect — Disabling every background service is neither required nor the time-sharing/resource-allocation mechanism described.

C · correct — Scheduling/time-sharing interleave useful work and can allocate service according to the system’s fairness policy.

D · incorrect — Manual intervention for each task adds administration rather than explaining efficient automatic CPU allocation.

Question 185

Which type of OS is designed to process large numbers of similar jobs without user interaction during execution?

  • A. Real-Time OS
  • B. Time-Sharing OS
  • C. Batch System
  • D. Single-User OS

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 2

Compare interaction, CPU sharing and deadline requirements under the stated system model.

OS02 · Batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing and real time

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS02 · Batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing and real time

Correct answer: C — Batch system.

OS02 defines batch jobs as running with little interaction after submission. That matches processing submitted similar jobs without user interaction during execution. Time-sharing targets interactive users, real-time targets timing correctness, and single-user classifies concurrent access rather than job interaction. The job-processing clue therefore selects batch rather than a user-count or deadline category.

Evidence in the complete study note

Batch jobs run with little interaction after submission. Multiprogramming keeps several jobs available so another can use the CPU while one waits; it does not require fixed time slices. Time-sharing uses rapid scheduling/preemption to support interactive users.OS02
Single-user and multiuser describe concurrent user access, not the number of applications. Network systems expose remote services; distributed systems coordinate work across machines, sometimes hiding location. Real-time correctness includes timing: hard deadlines must be met, while soft deadline misses degrade service.OS02

C · correct — Batch processing runs submitted jobs with little interaction, directly matching the scenario.

D · incorrect — Single-user describes concurrent user access, not whether a workload is submitted and processed as batches.

Question 186

In a Process Control Block (PCB), what does the Program Counter store?

  • A. The process priority level
  • B. The address of the next instruction to be executed for that process
  • C. The amount of CPU time used
  • D. The list of open files

Source

Distinguish stored instructions, an executing instance and its saved execution state.

Study-guide sections

OS05 · Programs, processes and PCB contents

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS05 · Programs, processes and PCB contents

Correct answer: B — The saved next-instruction position for resuming the process.

OS05 says the PCB records PC/registers alongside scheduling, memory/accounting and I/O information, and a saved PC identifies where execution resumes. Thus B describes its specific field role. Priority, CPU usage and open files can be other PCB information, but they are not the program-counter value. The question requires distinguishing fields within one record rather than excluding them from the PCB entirely.

Evidence in the complete study note

A saved PC identifies where execution resumes.OS05

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Priority is scheduling information in the PCB, not the saved next-instruction position held by PC.

B · correct — The saved PC records the instruction position at which the process will resume execution.

C · incorrect — CPU time consumed is accounting information rather than the program-counter address.

D · incorrect — Open files belong to I/O/open-resource state, not to the saved instruction-pointer field.

Question 187

Which process state indicates that a process is waiting for an I/O event to complete?

  • A. Ready
  • B. Running
  • C. Waiting
  • D. Terminated

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 4

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Decide whether the process is eligible, running or waiting, then identify the responsible queue or scheduling role.

Study-guide sections

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Correct answer: C — Waiting/blocked.

OS06 defines waiting/blocked as unable to proceed until an event and maps blocking I/O from running to waiting. Therefore a process waiting for I/O completion is in Waiting. It is not ready while the event prevents progress, not running during the wait, and not terminated merely because its request is outstanding. Completion normally makes it ready afterward.

Evidence in the complete study note

New processes are created/admitted; ready means eligible for CPU but not currently running. Running uses a CPU. Waiting/blocked means unable to proceed until an event; terminated means execution has ended. Running→ready follows time-slice expiry or another preemption. Running→waiting follows blocking I/O. I/O completion normally gives waiting→ready, not a guaranteed immediate running state.OS06

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Ready means eligible for CPU immediately; the outstanding blocking I/O prevents that eligibility.

B · incorrect — Running means currently using a CPU, unlike the blocked interval awaiting I/O completion.

C · correct — Waiting/blocked is the state for a process that cannot proceed until its I/O event completes.

D · incorrect — Terminated means execution has ended, not temporarily paused pending completion of an I/O request.

Question 188

What is the primary purpose of a Context Switch?

  • A. To permanently stop a process
  • B. To save the state of the current process and restore the state of the next scheduled process
  • C. To allocate more memory to a process
  • D. To terminate a low-priority process

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 5

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish stored instructions, an executing instance and its saved execution state.

Study-guide sections

OS05 · Programs, processes and PCB contents

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS05 · Programs, processes and PCB contents

Correct answer: B — Save one execution context and restore another.

OS05 directly defines a context switch as saving one execution context and restoring another, with overhead interrupting useful application work. B matches. A process may later resume, so switching does not permanently stop or kill it. Allocating memory is another management operation, and a privilege transition alone need not switch processes.

Evidence in the complete study note

A context switch saves one execution context and restores another; useful application work is interrupted by this overhead. An interrupt or system call can switch privilege without switching process.OS05

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The outgoing process can resume later; permanent stopping is termination rather than context switching.

B · correct — Saving current state and restoring the selected execution context is the defined context-switch purpose.

C · incorrect — Memory allocation changes resource assignment, not the saved/restored execution context defining a switch.

D · incorrect — A context switch need not terminate any process, regardless of priority.

Question 189

Which scheduler is responsible for selecting which process from the ready queue should be executed next on the CPU?

  • A. Long-Term Scheduler
  • B. Medium-Term Scheduler
  • C. Short-Term (CPU) Scheduler
  • D. I/O Scheduler

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 6

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Decide whether the process is eligible, running or waiting, then identify the responsible queue or scheduling role.

Study-guide sections

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Correct answer: C — Short-term (CPU) scheduler.

OS06 assigns admission to the long-term scheduler, suspension/swapping to the medium-term scheduler and ready-work selection to the short-term scheduler. The question specifically asks which ready process receives the CPU next, so C is exact. The dispatcher then performs the handoff; that is distinct from selecting the ready process. Device request ordering is not CPU ready-queue selection.

Evidence in the complete study note

The ready queue contains eligible work; device queues hold blocked requests. The broad textbook job queue contains processes in the system, while an admission queue more narrowly holds jobs awaiting memory/admission. Long-term scheduling admits jobs; medium-term scheduling suspends/swaps them; short-term scheduling selects ready work. The dispatcher performs the handoff/context restore after selection.OS06

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The long-term scheduler admits jobs rather than making each immediate ready-queue CPU selection.

B · incorrect — The medium-term scheduler suspends/swaps work rather than choosing the next CPU-ready process.

C · correct — The short-term CPU scheduler selects the next eligible process from ready work.

D · incorrect — An I/O scheduler orders device work rather than selecting the next process for the CPU.

OS27 · Address binding, dynamic loading and linking
