Question 191

A Race Condition occurs when:

  • A. Two processes finish execution at the same time
  • B. Multiple processes access shared resources simultaneously without proper synchronization, causing unpredictable results
  • C. A high-priority process always runs before a low-priority one
  • D. A process is preempted by the OS scheduler

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 8

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Identify conflicting shared accesses and the safety or progress property required.

Study-guide sections

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

Correct answer: B — Uncoordinated conflicting shared accesses make results depend on interleaving.

OS10 defines a race by results depending on uncoordinated interleaving of conflicting accesses. B is the intended match, with shared access understood to include a conflict such as an update; simultaneous read-only access alone is insufficient. Coincident completion, ordinary priority selection and preemption do not by themselves establish a shared-state race.

Evidence in the complete study note

A race occurs when results depend on uncoordinated interleaving of conflicting accesses.OS10
Two threads read counter=0, each computes 1 and each stores 1. Two increments produce 1 instead of 2. Protecting the entire read-modify-write with one lock prevents that lost update.OS10

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Two completions at the same time need not involve conflicting shared accesses or interleaving-dependent results.

B · correct — Unsynchronized conflicting shared accesses can make results depend on execution interleaving, which is the race condition.

C · incorrect — Priority order alone is a scheduling choice and does not establish a conflicting shared-data race.

D · incorrect — Preemption can expose a race but is not itself sufficient without conflicting unprotected accesses.

Question 192

Which of the following is NOT a necessary condition for deadlock?

  • A. Mutual Exclusion
  • B. Hold and Wait
  • C. Preemption
  • D. Circular Wait

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 9

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

Study-guide sections

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: C — Preemption is not a necessary condition; no-preemption is.

OS22 names mutual exclusion, hold-and-wait, no resource preemption and circular wait as the necessary set. The option Preemption reverses one of those conditions. Therefore C is the exception, while the other three are genuine members. This resource-preemption rule is separate from whether CPU scheduling uses timer preemption.

Evidence in the complete study note

Mutual exclusion allows at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. Hold-and-wait means retaining a resource while requesting another. No resource preemption means release must be voluntary. Circular wait forms a closed chain of such waits.OS22
All four are necessary in the standard reusable-resource model.OS22

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Mutual exclusion is explicitly one of the four necessary conditions in the standard resource model.

B · incorrect — Hold-and-wait is explicitly necessary: a process retains a resource while requesting another.

C · correct — The required condition is no-preemption; permitting preemption is not one of the necessary conditions.

D · incorrect — Circular wait is explicitly a required closed dependency chain in the standard model.

Question 193

All three processes arrive at time 0 and are ready immediately. Their CPU burst times are P1 = 24 ms, P2 = 3 ms and P3 = 3 ms. Using non-preemptive FCFS with tie order P1, P2, P3, and ignoring context-switch overhead, what is the average waiting time?

  • A. 3 ms
  • B. 9 ms
  • C. 17 ms
  • D. 27 ms

Adapted from

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 10

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Follow arrival order, include idle periods and distinguish waiting from completion time.

Study-guide sections

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

Correct answer: C — 17 ms.

FCFS runs P1 from 0 to 24 ms, P2 from 24 to 27 ms, and P3 from 27 to 30 ms. Because every process arrives at 0, their waiting times are 0, 24 and 27 ms. The average is (0+24+27)/3 = 51/3 = 17 ms, so C is correct. The average turnaround is a different quantity: (24+27+30)/3 = 27 ms.

Evidence in the complete study note

FCFS runs ready bursts in arrival order and ordinarily does not preempt.OS15
All ready at time 0, tie order P1=24,P2=3,P3=3: schedule 0–24 P1,24–27 P2,27–30 P3. Waiting times 0,24,27 average to 17. Turnarounds 24,27,30 average to 27. If arrivals are not simultaneous, recompute using their actual times.OS15
For arrival A, first start S and completion C, response=S−A and turnaround=C−A. For a single CPU burst B with no I/O, waiting=C−A−B.OS13

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — 3 ms is the burst time of P2 or P3, not the average ready-queue wait.

B · incorrect — The three waits total 51 ms, so dividing by three cannot give 9 ms.

C · correct — The waits 0, 24 and 27 ms average to 17 ms.

D · incorrect — 27 ms is the average turnaround time in this example, not the average waiting time.

Question 194

What problem does the 'Aging' technique solve in Priority Scheduling?

  • A. Deadlock
  • B. Starvation of low-priority processes
  • C. Race conditions
  • D. Memory fragmentation

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 11

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check priority direction, preemption and whether waiting can change effective rank.

Study-guide sections

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

OS29 · Internal and external fragmentation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

OS29 · Internal and external fragmentation

Correct answer: B — Starvation of low-priority processes.

OS17 defines aging as increasing waiting jobs’ effective priority to reduce indefinite postponement under suitable rules/workloads. Thus B names the problem it addresses. It does not remove a deadlock dependency cycle, coordinate conflicting memory updates or consolidate fragmented memory. The result still depends on the priority/aging policy; naming aging alone does not provide an unconditional deadline guarantee.

Evidence in the complete study note

Aging increases waiting jobs’ effective priority according to a rule, reducing indefinite postponement when the priority scheme and workload permit. It is not a deadlock cure.OS17
A race occurs when results depend on uncoordinated interleaving of conflicting accesses.OS10
Internal fragmentation is unused space inside an allocated unit. External fragmentation is sufficient total free space split into holes that cannot satisfy a required contiguous allocation.OS29

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Aging changes priority rather than breaking the blocked resource dependencies of a deadlock.

B · correct — Increasing effective priority of waiting low-priority jobs addresses their starvation under sustained higher-priority load.

C · incorrect — Races require shared-access synchronization; priority aging is not a mutual-exclusion mechanism.

D · incorrect — Memory fragmentation concerns allocation layout rather than the priority of waiting CPU jobs.

Question 195

Which scheduling algorithm is known as preemptive SJF?

  • A. Round Robin
  • B. FCFS
  • C. Shortest Remaining Time First (SRTF)
  • D. Priority Scheduling

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 12

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare the ready jobs using the stated burst or remaining-time rule.

Study-guide sections

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS16 · SJF/SJN and shortest remaining time

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

Correct answer: C — Shortest Remaining Time First.

OS16 distinguishes non-preemptive SJF/SJN from SRTF, which reconsiders at arrivals and can preempt for shorter remaining work. Therefore SRTF is the preemptive SJF variant. RR uses fixed cyclic turns, FCFS arrival order and generic priority an independently defined ranking. The original burst length and the remaining length must not be confused after a job has executed.

Evidence in the complete study note

Non-preemptive SJF/SJN chooses the shortest next burst among currently ready jobs. SRTF reconsiders on arrivals and selects the shortest remaining work; a shorter newcomer can preempt. Future burst lengths normally need estimates.OS16
RR serves a ready queue cyclically for at most quantum q per turn.OS18
FCFS runs ready bursts in arrival order and ordinarily does not preempt.OS15

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — RR selects cyclic quantum turns rather than the shortest remaining CPU work.

B · incorrect — FCFS follows arrival order and is ordinarily non-preemptive rather than preemptive shortest-job scheduling.

C · correct — SRTF chooses minimum remaining work and can interrupt a running job for a shorter arrival.

D · incorrect — Generic priority scheduling does not specifically rank by remaining burst length and can be either variant.

Question 196

In Round Robin scheduling, what is the key challenge?

  • A. Managing priority levels
  • B. Determining the appropriate time quantum length
  • C. Avoiding deadlocks
  • D. Allocating memory to processes

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 13

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace bounded turns in ready-queue order and include the stated overhead and arrivals.

Study-guide sections

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

Correct answer: B — Choose an appropriate time quantum.

OS18 says a large RR quantum approaches FCFS, while tiny quanta increase context-switch overhead. Selecting q therefore trades responsiveness against overhead, matching B. Priority management, deadlock policy and memory allocation are separate concerns rather than the defining parameter-selection challenge of ordinary Round Robin. The useful quantum depends on workloads and switching cost.

Evidence in the complete study note

A large quantum approaches FCFS; tiny quanta increase switch overhead.OS18
With n continuously ready jobs, no priority bypass and zero overhead, a just-requeued job waits at most (n−1)q for its next turn. This bounds one interval, not total lifetime waiting when arrivals or blocking are unrestricted. Include context-switch costs if given.OS18

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Basic RR uses cyclic ready-queue turns rather than requiring a hierarchy of priority levels.

B · correct — Quantum length controls responsiveness and switching overhead, creating RR’s characteristic tuning tradeoff.

C · incorrect — Deadlock avoidance concerns resources and dependencies, not the defining RR quantum parameter.

D · incorrect — Memory allocation is a separate management problem rather than the Round Robin selection rule’s main parameter.

Question 197

What is a Semaphore?

  • A. A scheduling algorithm
  • B. A memory management technique
  • C. A synchronization variable that controls access to shared resources in a multi-process environment
  • D. A type of file allocation method

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 14

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Track available permits and blocked operations using the stated semaphore model.

Study-guide sections

OS11 · Semaphores and resource-count traces

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS28 · Contiguous allocation and fit policies

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS11 · Semaphores and resource-count traces

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS28 · Contiguous allocation and fit policies

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Correct answer: C — A synchronization variable with atomic wait and signal operations.

OS11 defines a semaphore through atomic wait/P/down and signal/V/up operations. Tokens control whether participants can proceed with shared resource work, with binary or counting forms. C identifies that synchronization role. Scheduling, memory-management and file-allocation policies solve different resource-selection/layout problems and do not define semaphore semantics.

Evidence in the complete study note

A semaphore provides atomic wait/P/down and signal/V/up operations. Under the nonnegative-token model, wait consumes one available token or blocks when none is available; signal supplies a token and may wake a waiter. Implementations using negative internal counts encode waiters differently, so state the model. A binary semaphore represents one permit; a counting semaphore tracks several. A mutex normally has ownership rules that a general semaphore lacks.OS11
FCFS runs ready bursts in arrival order and ordinarily does not preempt.OS15
A contiguous allocation gives a process one continuous region.OS28
Contiguous allocation uses consecutive blocks; logical block i is start+i and supports direct access, but growth and external fragmentation are problems.OS38

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — A semaphore synchronizes participants; it is not itself a CPU-selection scheduling algorithm.

B · incorrect — It controls coordination through permits, rather than choosing memory locations or translation layouts.

C · correct — Atomic wait/signal on a synchronization variable controls permits for concurrent shared-resource operations.

D · incorrect — File allocation chooses storage-block layout; that is separate from semaphore synchronization semantics.

Question 198

What is the difference between a Binary Semaphore and a Counting Semaphore?

  • A. Binary semaphores are faster
  • B. Binary semaphores have only two states (0 or 1), while counting semaphores track available resources up to a maximum count
  • C. Counting semaphores are used only for deadlock prevention

Track available permits and blocked operations using the stated semaphore model.

OS11 · Semaphores and resource-count traces

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS11 · Semaphores and resource-count traces

Correct answer: B — Binary tracks one permit; counting tracks several.

OS11 defines a binary semaphore as one permit and a counting semaphore as several, under the nonnegative-token model. Thus binary availability is 0/1 while a resource pool can have a larger count. B states this logical distinction. Internal negative-waiter encodings are implementation details, not a reason to claim speed guarantees, deadlock-only usage or no difference.

Evidence in the complete study note

A semaphore provides atomic wait/P/down and signal/V/up operations. Under the nonnegative-token model, wait consumes one available token or blocks when none is available; signal supplies a token and may wake a waiter. Implementations using negative internal counts encode waiters differently, so state the model. A binary semaphore represents one permit; a counting semaphore tracks several. A mutex normally has ownership rules that a general semaphore lacks.OS11

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The note gives no universal relative-speed guarantee; permit capacity is the defining distinction.

B · correct — In the nonnegative-token model, binary availability is zero/one while counting semaphores track several available permits.

C · incorrect — Counting semaphores coordinate resource pools and are not limited to preventing deadlock.

D · incorrect — The allowed permit count is a substantive difference explicitly stated here.

Question 199

What must hold simultaneously for a deadlock to occur?

  • A. Only Mutual Exclusion and Circular Wait
  • B. Any two of the four conditions
  • C. All four necessary conditions: Mutual Exclusion, Hold-and-Wait, No Preemption, and Circular Wait
  • D. Only Circular Wait

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 16

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace the resource dependencies and distinguish necessary conditions from evidence of an actual deadlock.

Study-guide sections

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS22 · Deadlock and its four necessary conditions

Correct answer: C — All four standard necessary conditions.

OS22 says all four conditions are necessary in the standard reusable-resource model. C names that full set. Any two or a cycle alone does not state the complete conjunction. This is a necessary-condition answer, not an assertion that merely allowing all four policies proves a deadlock already exists in a particular allocation state.

Evidence in the complete study note

Mutual exclusion allows at most one holder of a nonshareable resource. Hold-and-wait means retaining a resource while requesting another. No resource preemption means release must be voluntary. Circular wait forms a closed chain of such waits.OS22
All four are necessary in the standard reusable-resource model. Their availability as general system policies does not prove a deadlock exists at this instant; inspect actual allocations/requests.OS22

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — It omits hold-and-wait and no-preemption, both necessary in the stated standard resource model.

B · incorrect — An arbitrary pair does not supply all four necessary restrictions for the standard deadlock model.

C · correct — This option lists mutual exclusion, hold-and-wait, no-preemption and circular wait together.

D · incorrect — A cycle alone omits other necessary restrictions; it is not the complete standard condition set.

Question 200

In a Resource Allocation Graph (RAG), what does a cycle with single-instance resources indicate?

  • A. Potential deadlock only
  • B. No deadlock possible
  • C. Definite deadlock
  • D. High CPU utilization

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 17

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Follow request and assignment arrows and check how many instances each resource has.

Study-guide sections

OS23 · Resource graphs and missing-diagram limits

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS23 · Resource graphs and missing-diagram limits

Correct answer: C — Definite deadlock under single-instance resource assumptions.

OS23 says a cycle implies deadlock when every resource type has one instance. The stem supplies that decisive assumption, so C follows. With multiple instances a cycle would indicate only a possibility, but importing that different model would ignore the literal question. A graph cycle also says nothing by itself about high CPU utilization.

Evidence in the complete study note

In a resource-allocation graph, P→R is a request and an instance R→P is an assignment. Follow these directions to form waits. No cycle implies no deadlock; with one instance of every resource type, a cycle implies deadlock. With multiple instances a cycle alone is insufficient: another instance may permit completion. A wait-for graph replaces single-instance resource nodes with P→Q when P waits for Q.OS23

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Potential-only applies to the more general multiple-instance caveat, not the supplied single-instance case.

B · incorrect — A cycle in the stated single-instance model establishes deadlock rather than excluding it.

C · correct — The note explicitly gives cycle implies deadlock for a graph with one instance per resource type.

D · incorrect — Resource dependency cycles do not measure CPU busy time or establish high processor utilization.

Question 201

Which deadlock handling method allows deadlock to occur but then recovers from it?

  • A. Deadlock Prevention
  • B. Deadlock Ignorance
  • C. Deadlock Recovery
  • D. Deadlock Avoidance

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 18

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Separate handling before allocation from actions taken after detection, and consider the cost of checking.

Study-guide sections

OS26 · Detection, recovery and intentional ignoring

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS26 · Detection, recovery and intentional ignoring

OS24 · Prevention: identify the condition removed

OS25 · Avoidance and the Banker safety test

Correct answer: C — Deadlock recovery.

OS26 describes recovery through terminating selected processes or safely reclaiming resources and rolling work back after deadlock is detected. That matches allowing occurrence and then resolving it. Prevention removes a necessary condition, avoidance preserves safety before granting, and ignoring accepts risk without committing to recovery. The complete handling strategy is detection plus recovery; C is the offered recovery component.

Evidence in the complete study note

Detection asks whether the current state is deadlocked. Recovery can terminate selected processes or reclaim safely preemptable resources and roll affected work back to a checkpoint. Termination and rollback are both possible recovery tools; choose according to the resource and process conditions.OS26
Prevention constrains how requests are made.OS24
Avoidance allows requests only if the resulting state remains safe.OS25
Ignoring a sufficiently rare problem is sometimes called the ostrich policy, but real systems still prevent or detect selected deadlocks.OS26

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Prevention constrains requests so the targeted deadlock class cannot arise rather than accepting occurrence first.

B · incorrect — Ignoring rare deadlock does not define active detection and subsequent recovery.

C · correct — Recovery acts on an actual detected deadlock using termination, reclamation or rollback as appropriate.

D · incorrect — Avoidance checks safe states before grants rather than deliberately permitting deadlock and then repairing it.

Question 202

What is Address Binding at 'Load Time'?

  • A. Addresses are fixed by the compiler before execution

OS27 · Address binding, dynamic loading and linking

Correct answer: C — Relocate addresses when the program is loaded.

OS27 defines load-time binding as relocation when placed into memory. That matches C’s final-address assignment at load when placement was unknown at compile time. Fixing absolute addresses during compilation is compile-time binding; translating while running is execution-time binding. Merely storing address bytes on a disk describes neither the placement decision nor its timing.

Evidence in the complete study note

Compile-time binding uses absolute addresses if placement is known. Load-time binding relocates when placed into memory. Execution-time binding translates while running and permits movement with hardware support.OS27

D · incorrect — Storing address information on disk does not define when placement-dependent address binding occurs.

Question 203

Under basic page-frame allocation and variable-segment allocation, which statement correctly contrasts allocation-unit size and external fragmentation?

  • A. Paging uses variable-sized blocks; segmentation uses fixed-sized blocks
  • B. Paging divides memory into fixed-sized pages eliminating external fragmentation; segmentation uses variable-sized logical segments but can cause external fragmentation
  • C. Segmentation is used only in virtual memory
  • D. Paging requires no hardware support

Adapted from

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 20

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check the logical region and offset bound before computing its physical location.

Study-guide sections

OS31 · Segmentation and contrasts with paging

OS30 · Paging, page tables and TLB

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS31 · Segmentation and contrasts with paging

OS30 · Paging, page tables and TLB

Correct answer: B — Fixed-size paging avoids external page-frame fragmentation; variable segments can fragment externally.

Paging maps fixed-size pages into equal-size frames, avoiding external fragmentation of frame allocation, although internal waste can remain. Segmentation uses variable-length logical regions and can leave external holes. This is the unit-size and fragmentation contrast in B. A reverses the sizes. C does not supply that contrast, and D substitutes a hardware-support claim for it; page translation still requires mappings and checks.

Evidence in the complete study note

Under the basic models, paging uses fixed units/page tables and can waste space internally; segmentation uses variable logical units/segment tables and can fragment externally. Segments reflect program structure whereas ordinary pages are largely transparent to applications.OS31
Pages and physical frames have equal fixed size P. Divide virtual address v into page=floor(v/P) and offset=v mod P. The page table maps a page to a frame and protection/status flags. Physical address=frame×P+offset.OS30

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The sizes are reversed: pages are fixed-size units and segments are variable-length regions.

B · correct — This gives the required fixed-page versus variable-segment size and external-fragmentation contrast.

C · incorrect — An exclusive-use statement does not compare allocation-unit sizes or external fragmentation as requested.

D · incorrect — This does not give the requested size/fragmentation contrast; paging also uses translation mappings and checks.

OS29 · Internal and external fragmentation

Correct answer: B — Free capacity is split across holes too small for a required contiguous allocation.

OS29 defines external fragmentation as sufficient total free space split into holes unable to satisfy a contiguous allocation. B matches that definition, with large requests understood to require one continuous region. A instead describes internal fragmentation. Question 205

What is the purpose of the Page Table in Paging?

Question 206

OS31 · Segmentation and contrasts with paging

Correct answer: B — Physical address = segment base + valid offset.

Evidence in the complete study note

Segmentation uses logical variable-length units such as code, stack and arrays. A segment-table entry provides base, limit and permissions. Question 208

In demand paging, assume the accessed virtual page is valid, permitted and backed by disk, but its contents are not resident and must be read from disk. What event is the page fault in this scenario?

  • A. A hardware memory error
  • B. When the CPU tries to access a page not currently in physical memory, requiring the OS to load it from disk
  • C. An error in the page table
  • D. When a process exceeds its allocated memory

Adapted from

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 25

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Separate a permitted nonresident access from an illegal access and trace the required handling steps.

Study-guide sections

OS32 · Virtual memory and page-fault handling

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS32 · Virtual memory and page-fault handling

Correct answer: B — When the CPU tries to access a page not currently in physical memory, requiring the OS to load it from disk

A valid nonresident access traps so the OS can obtain the page contents, update the mapping and restart the access. The specified page is disk-backed and requires a read, making B accurate for this scenario. Page faults in general need not read disk; zero-fill and shared mappings are other cases. Nothing here requires hardware failure, table corruption or exceeding the permitted address range.

Evidence in the complete study note

Demand paging brings a page in when accessed. On a nonresident access: trap, validate mapping/permissions, find a frame or choose a victim, save a dirty victim if needed, obtain contents, update mapping and restart.OS32
Not every fault reads a disk: some allocate zero-filled pages or establish shared mappings. An illegal access is rejected, not automatically loaded.OS32

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The trap does not imply damaged memory hardware.

B · correct — The valid disk-backed page is nonresident, so this access requires the stated page-in.

C · incorrect — A correct table may intentionally mark a valid page nonresident.

D · incorrect — The page is explicitly within the valid permitted mapping, so no allocation violation is implied.

Question 209

What is Demand Paging?

  • A. Loading the entire program into memory before execution
  • B. Loading only the pages that are actually needed during program execution, keeping others on disk until required
  • C. Allocating memory in fixed partitions
  • D. Swapping entire processes between memory and disk

OS32 · Virtual memory and page-fault handling

OS27 · Address binding, dynamic loading and linking

OS28 · Contiguous allocation and fit policies

Correct answer: B — Bring a page into memory when it is needed/accessed.

OS32 defines demand paging as bringing a page in when accessed. B matches loading only the pages demanded during execution, with disk backing for the described stored portions. Whole-program loading, fixed contiguous partitions and whole-process swapping do not name this per-page demand policy.

Question 210

What is the difference between Internal and External Fragmentation?

  • A. Both are the same type of memory waste
  • B. Internal fragmentation wastes space inside allocated partitions (allocated > needed); external fragmentation wastes space between allocations
  • C. External fragmentation occurs in paging systems
  • D. Internal fragmentation only occurs in virtual memory

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 27

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Locate unused space relative to an allocated unit and distinguish total free space from contiguous free space.

Study-guide sections

OS29 · Internal and external fragmentation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS29 · Internal and external fragmentation

Correct answer: B — Internal waste is inside allocations; external holes are between them.

OS29 defines internal fragmentation as unused space inside an allocated unit and external fragmentation as free holes unable to serve a required contiguous request. B states the location distinction. C is not the general definition and needs allocator scope; D adds an unsupported virtual-memory-only restriction. These wastes can arise from allocation geometry rather than the program’s CPU behavior.

Evidence in the complete study note

Internal fragmentation is unused space inside an allocated unit. External fragmentation is sufficient total free space split into holes that cannot satisfy a required contiguous allocation.OS29
Basic fixed-frame paging avoids external fragmentation of page-frame allocation but can waste the final page internally.OS29

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The note explicitly distinguishes internal unused allocation space from external free-hole fragmentation.

B · correct — Internal space is wasted within an allocation; external waste is free space split between allocations.

C · incorrect — This does not define the contrast; basic page-frame allocation avoids external fragmentation, though other allocators may differ.

D · incorrect — Unused space within an allocated unit is not limited by definition to virtual-memory systems.

Question 211

Which memory allocation strategy picks the first hole large enough to accommodate a process?

  • A. Best-Fit
  • B. Worst-Fit
  • C. First-Fit
  • D. Next-Fit

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 28

Apply the stated hole-selection or base-and-bound rule with its explicit convention.

Study-guide sections

OS28 · Contiguous allocation and fit policies

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS28 · Contiguous allocation and fit policies

Correct answer: C — First-fit.

OS28 defines first-fit as selecting the first adequate hole. This is exactly the stem’s first hole large enough. Best-fit searches for the smallest adequate hole, worst-fit for the largest, and next-fit resumes from a prior search position. The explicit first-hole condition selects first-fit without any numeric hole trace.

Evidence in the complete study note

First-fit selects the first adequate hole; next-fit continues from the previous search position; best-fit selects the smallest adequate hole; worst-fit selects the largest.OS28

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Best-fit minimizes adequate hole size rather than stopping at the first adequate hole.

B · incorrect — Worst-fit selects the largest available hole rather than the first adequate one encountered.

C · correct — First-fit stops at the first hole that can accommodate the requested allocation.

D · incorrect — Next-fit continues from the previous search position; the stem gives the ordinary first-fit criterion.

Question 212

What is Swapping in OS memory management?

  • A. Exchanging CPU registers between processes

OS27 · Address binding, dynamic loading and linking

OS05 · Programs, processes and PCB contents

Correct answer: B — Temporarily move a whole process to backing store.

OS27 explicitly distinguishes traditional swapping of a whole process to backing store from paging individual pages and context switching execution state. B describes the whole-process memory-management operation. Saving registers is a context-switch task, file copying is a filesystem operation, and replacing instruction contents does not define swapping’s residence change.

Swapping traditionally moves a whole process to backing store; paging moves individual pages. These differ from a context switch, which primarily changes execution state.OS27

Question 213

What is Dynamic Loading?

  • A. Loading the entire program into memory at startup
  • B. Loading routines from disk into memory only when they are actually called during execution
  • C. Dynamically changing the priority of a process
  • D. Moving pages between RAM and disk automatically

OS27 · Address binding, dynamic loading and linking

OS32 · Virtual memory and page-fault handling

OS17 · Priority, starvation and aging

Correct answer: B — Load a routine only when needed.

OS27 directly defines dynamic loading as bringing a routine in when needed. B states the routine-on-call form of that rule. Eager startup loading is the opposite, priority changes are scheduling, and automatic page movement is a virtual-memory mechanism at a different granularity. Dynamic loading concerns when code routines become resident, not which process gets CPU priority.

Dynamic loading brings a routine in when needed.OS27

Question 214

What is the key management-layer distinction between user-level threads and kernel-level threads, without assuming universal speed or reliability advantages?

  • A. User-level threads are managed by a user-space library; kernel-level threads are known to and managed by the OS
  • B. Kernel-level threads are always faster
  • C. User-level threads can access hardware directly
  • D. There is no practical difference between them

Adapted from

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 31

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Compare per-thread execution context with shared process resources and the stated thread mapping.

Study-guide sections

OS08 · Threads and mapping models

OS-S02 · Thread management costs and execution privilege

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS08 · Threads and mapping models

OS-S02 · Thread management costs and execution privilege

Correct answer: A — User-level threads are managed by a user-space library; kernel-level threads are known to and managed by the OS

User-level threads are managed by a library, while kernel threads are execution contexts known to the OS. Their mapping affects blocking behavior and use of multiple CPUs. The management layer alone does not guarantee faster creation, faster execution or greater stability; those depend on implementation. It also does not grant user code privileged hardware access. The option keeps the supported management distinction.

Evidence in the complete study note

User-level threads are managed by a library; kernel threads are known to the OS. In many-to-one mapping, a blocking kernel call can block all user threads and one kernel execution context cannot use multiple CPUs simultaneously. One-to-one permits another kernel thread to run when one blocks. Many-to-many multiplexes user threads over several kernel threads. Speed and reliability depend on implementation; the management layer alone guarantees neither. Application names such as browser or Excel do not prove a particular version’s threading model.OS08
Being scheduled or managed by the kernel does not give application thread code kernel privilege. Ordinary applications and supporting utilities execute in restricted user mode and request protected services through libraries and system calls. Kernel component code executes in privileged kernel mode and performs permitted low-level hardware operations.OS-S02

Choice by choice

A · correct — This states library management versus OS-known kernel threads.

B · incorrect — No management layer guarantees that kernel threads are always faster.

C · incorrect — User-level thread management does not grant direct privileged hardware access.

D · incorrect — Blocking and CPU-use differences show that the distinction has practical effects.

Question 215

What is IPC (Inter-Process Communication)?

  • A. A CPU scheduling algorithm
  • B. Mechanisms allowing processes to exchange information, synchronize, and coordinate activities
  • C. A method for allocating memory between processes

Study-guide sections

OS09 · IPC and blocking semantics

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS09 · IPC and blocking semantics

Correct answer: B — Mechanisms for exchanging information and coordinating processes.

OS09 describes shared-memory communication, send/receive, pipes, queues, sockets and RPC, including synchronization for conflicting updates. B summarizes the exchange and coordination role. IPC is not a CPU selection algorithm or a particular memory-allocation/file operation, even though shared memory and file-based mechanisms may be used to implement it.

Evidence in the complete study note

Shared memory lets processes access a common region and requires synchronization for conflicting updates. Message passing exchanges data through send/receive operations. Pipes provide streams; message queues preserve messages; sockets connect endpoints; RPC requests a remote operation. HTTP is an application protocol over a communication mechanism and can carry interprocess communication, so it is not universally “not IPC.”OS09
File locks coordinate access rather than transfer arbitrary payloads, though locking is synchronization within IPC.OS09

A · incorrect — CPU scheduling selects execution opportunities, rather than defining communication and coordination among processes.

C · incorrect — Shared memory can implement IPC, but memory allocation alone does not define interprocess communication.

Question 216

What is the Critical Section problem concerned with?

  • A. Preventing processes from using too much CPU
  • B. Ensuring that no two processes are executing their critical sections (code accessing shared resources) simultaneously
  • C. Managing disk I/O operations
  • D. Scheduling processes in real-time systems

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 33

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Identify conflicting shared accesses and the safety or progress property required.

Study-guide sections

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS10 · Races, critical sections and correctness

Correct answer: B — Mutually exclude conflicting critical-region execution.

OS10 defines a critical section as code accessing shared state needing protection and lists mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting as requirements. B states the exclusion aspect for critical sections protecting the same conflicting resource. The problem also includes progress/fair-waiting requirements, so exclusion is not its entire specification. CPU quotas, disk management and real-time scheduling are different topics.

Evidence in the complete study note

A race occurs when results depend on uncoordinated interleaving of conflicting accesses. A critical section is code accessing shared state that needs protection, not the resource itself. A correct exclusion solution requires mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting under stated assumptions. Mutual exclusion is a desired safety property; races, starvation and deadlock are possible failures.OS10
Keep critical regions small and ensure every conflicting access uses the same protection. A barrier waits until a defined set of participants reaches a point; a monitor encapsulates protected operations with condition synchronization. Locking can itself introduce deadlock if acquisition order is inconsistent.OS10

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — CPU consumption limits are not the shared-access exclusion problem defining critical sections.

B · correct — Conflicting critical regions must not execute simultaneously; this is the mutual-exclusion part of the correctness requirements.

C · incorrect — Disk I/O can involve shared state, but managing it generally is not the critical-section problem definition.

D · incorrect — Real-time selection addresses timing/scheduling, not the core shared-state exclusion requirement requested.

Question 217

For a general process that may perform I/O, which formula defines turnaround time from event timestamps? Waiting means time in the ready queue; burst means total CPU service. The formula must remain valid for nonzero arrival times and blocked intervals.

  • A. Burst Time - Waiting Time
  • B. Completion Time - Arrival Time
  • C. Start Time + Burst Time
  • D. Waiting Time + Burst Time

Adapted from

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 34

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Construct a timeline and distinguish completion rates from individual elapsed-time measures.

Study-guide sections

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

Correct answer: B — Completion Time - Arrival Time

Turnaround is completion time minus arrival time, including CPU execution, ready-queue waiting and blocked intervals. Ready waiting plus CPU service omits blocked time when I/O occurs. Start plus burst may resemble a completion timestamp for uninterrupted work, but it is not elapsed turnaround and does not subtract arrival. Burst minus waiting has no general elapsed-time interpretation. Only B remains valid in the full stated model.

Evidence in the complete study note

For arrival A, first start S and completion C, response=S−A and turnaround=C−A. For a single CPU burst B with no I/O, waiting=C−A−B. With I/O, subtract all CPU and blocked time to isolate ready-queue waiting.OS13

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Subtracting ready waiting from CPU service is not turnaround.

B · correct — Completion minus arrival includes the entire elapsed lifetime.

C · incorrect — Start plus burst is not generally completion with I/O and does not subtract arrival.

D · incorrect — Ready waiting plus CPU service omits blocked intervals.

Question 218

What is the Convoy Effect in FCFS scheduling?

  • A. High-priority processes always complete first
  • B. All processes receive equal CPU time
  • C. Short processes get stuck waiting behind one very long process, causing high average waiting times
  • D. Multiple processes share the same memory space

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 35

Follow arrival order, include idle periods and distinguish waiting from completion time.

Study-guide sections

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS08 · Threads and mapping models

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS15 · FCFS and the convoy effect

OS18 · Round Robin, quantum and bounded turns

OS08 · Threads and mapping models

Correct answer: C — Short jobs wait behind a long FCFS burst.

OS15 describes the convoy effect as long CPU-bound work making many short jobs wait under non-preemptive FCFS. C directly states that queueing effect and its high waiting-time consequence. FCFS does not use priority order or grant equal total CPU, and shared address space is unrelated to the arrival-order delay. The effect depends on a long burst preceding shorter ones.

Evidence in the complete study note

FCFS runs ready bursts in arrival order and ordinarily does not preempt. If the CPU is idle, jump to the next arrival. Long CPU-bound work can make many short jobs wait: the convoy effect.OS15
RR gives recurring opportunities, not identical total CPU consumption for jobs of different lengths.OS18
A thread has its own PC, registers and stack. Threads in a process share code, address space and usually open-file resources.OS08

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Priority order does not define FCFS, which follows arrival order rather than high-priority completion.

B · incorrect — FCFS serves entire bursts of possibly different lengths rather than enforcing equal CPU shares.

C · correct — Short jobs are delayed behind the long non-preempted burst, producing the FCFS convoy effect.

D · incorrect — Shared memory concerns address-space organization rather than the scheduling delay called a convoy.

Question 219

What is Cooperative (Non-Preemptive) vs Preemptive Scheduling?

  • A. They are the same thing
  • B. Non-preemptive: process runs until it voluntarily releases CPU; Preemptive: OS can forcibly take CPU from a running process for another
  • C. Preemptive scheduling never interrupts processes
  • D. Non-preemptive scheduling always gives better performance

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 36

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Check whether the running burst can be interrupted before completion or blocking.

Study-guide sections

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

OS21 · How to evaluate scheduling claims

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS14 · Preemptive versus non-preemptive decisions

OS21 · How to evaluate scheduling claims

Correct answer: B — Voluntary release versus possible forced CPU preemption.

OS14 says non-preemptive scheduling retains the CPU until completion, blocking or voluntary yielding, while preemptive scheduling may return running work to ready. B captures the voluntary-versus-forced distinction, with completion/blocking included among natural release events. C contradicts preemption, D claims an unsupported universal performance ranking, and A erases the defining difference.

Evidence in the complete study note

Non-preemptive scheduling keeps a running CPU burst until completion, blocking or voluntary yielding. Preemptive scheduling can return running work to ready, usually after a timer quantum or a more urgent arrival. FCFS and non-preemptive SJF are non-preemptive; RR and SRTF are preemptive. Priority can be either, so its name alone cannot decide. Cooperative describes relying on voluntary release.OS14
There is no universally best policy for response, waiting, fairness and throughput simultaneously.OS21

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The policies differ in whether a running CPU burst can be forcibly interrupted for another process.

B · correct — Non-preemptive work releases naturally/voluntarily; preemptive policy may forcibly reassign CPU execution time.

C · incorrect — Forcibly interrupting running work is exactly what preemptive scheduling permits.

D · incorrect — No policy universally performs better for all workloads and objectives, so the absolute performance claim fails.

Question 220

Which scheduling criterion measures the number of processes completed per unit of time?

  • A. CPU Utilization
  • B. Response Time
  • C. Throughput
  • D. Turnaround Time

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 37

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Construct a timeline and distinguish completion rates from individual elapsed-time measures.

Study-guide sections

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS13 · Scheduling metrics and calculation method

Correct answer: C — Throughput.

OS13 defines throughput as completed jobs divided by elapsed time. That exactly matches the stem’s process-completion rate. CPU utilization is busy-time fraction; response measures delay to first start, and turnaround delay to completion. The units therefore distinguish throughput from the other criteria without requiring a scheduling example.

Evidence in the complete study note

For arrival A, first start S and completion C, response=S−A and turnaround=C−A. For a single CPU burst B with no I/O, waiting=C−A−B. With I/O, subtract all CPU and blocked time to isolate ready-queue waiting. Throughput=completed jobs/elapsed time; CPU utilization=busy time/elapsed time. Minimize response/turnaround/waiting under the selected goal and maximize useful throughput/utilization.OS13

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — CPU utilization measures busy-time fraction, not how many processes finish per unit time.

B · incorrect — Response measures first-service delay for a process rather than a completion-count rate.

C · correct — Throughput is explicitly the number of completed jobs divided by elapsed time.

D · incorrect — Turnaround measures arrival-to-completion elapsed duration rather than completed jobs per time.

Question 221

What does the Job Queue contain?

  • A. Only processes currently running on the CPU
  • B. All processes currently in the system, including those not yet admitted to memory
  • C. Only processes waiting for I/O devices
  • D. Processes that have already terminated

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 38

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Study-guide sections

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS06 · Process states, queues and schedulers

Correct answer: B — All processes in the broad textbook system/job-queue sense.

OS06 explicitly defines the broad textbook job queue as processes in the system and distinguishes a narrower admission queue awaiting memory. B matches that broad usage, including not-yet-admitted jobs. Ready/running or I/O-blocked work are subsets, not the whole category; already-ended processes are not the intended current job population. The answer retains the note’s terminology qualification.

Evidence in the complete study note

The ready queue contains eligible work; device queues hold blocked requests. The broad textbook job queue contains processes in the system, while an admission queue more narrowly holds jobs awaiting memory/admission. Long-term scheduling admits jobs; medium-term scheduling suspends/swaps them; short-term scheduling selects ready work.OS06
New processes are created/admitted; ready means eligible for CPU but not currently running. Running uses a CPU. Waiting/blocked means unable to proceed until an event; terminated means execution has ended. Running→ready follows time-slice expiry or another preemption. Running→waiting follows blocking I/O. I/O completion normally gives waiting→ready, not a guaranteed immediate running state.OS06

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Currently running work is only a subset, not the broad population represented by the textbook job queue.

B · correct — The broad textbook usage includes system processes/jobs, including those awaiting memory admission.

C · incorrect — I/O waiting belongs to device queues and does not exhaust the broad job-queue population.

D · incorrect — Already-terminated work is not the current job population intended here’s broad system queue.

Question 222

In an open seekable file’s state, what does its current file-offset field (the file-position pointer, rather than the descriptor handle) track?

  • A. The physical location of the file on disk
  • B. The current position within an open file for the next read or write operation
  • C. The owner's access permissions
  • D. The file's creation timestamp

Adapted from

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 39

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Distinguish a file operation, a resource handle, an offset and file metadata.

Study-guide sections

OS36 · Files, descriptors and ordinary operations

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS36 · Files, descriptors and ordinary operations

Correct answer: B — The current position within an open file for the next read or write operation

The current offset records the logical position used for the next read or write on a seekable open file. A descriptor is instead a process-local integer handle referring to open-file state. Physical disk-block locations, permissions and creation timestamps describe other mapping or metadata information. By asking specifically about the position field, the revised stem makes B precise without conflating a descriptor with an offset.

Evidence in the complete study note

A file has content and metadata such as name, size, owner, permissions and timestamps. open obtains an open-file reference, read/write transfer bytes, seek changes an offset, close releases a reference, truncate changes size and unlink removes a name. A file descriptor is a process-local integer handle; the associated open-file state contains the current offset and access mode. Descriptor and offset are not synonyms.OS36
Read advances the offset on seekable files and may return fewer bytes than requested; zero normally indicates end-of-file.OS36

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Physical disk location is separate from the current logical I/O offset.

B · correct — The file-offset field records the position for the next read or write.

C · incorrect — Owner permissions are access-control metadata rather than the current position.

D · incorrect — Creation time is metadata rather than the current I/O offset.

Question 224

What is Contiguous Allocation of disk space?

  • A. Files are stored in scattered blocks linked by pointers
  • B. Each file occupies a continuous set of consecutive disk blocks, enabling fast sequential and direct access
  • C. Files are indexed using a separate index block
  • D. Files are allocated in fixed-size frames

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

OS30 · Paging, page tables and TLB

Correct answer: B — A file occupies consecutive disk blocks.

OS38 defines contiguous allocation through consecutive blocks, with logical block i at start+i. This permits sequential and direct access without following a chain. B matches. Scattered pointers describe linked allocation, a separate index describes indexed allocation, and fixed physical memory frames belong to paging rather than file-contiguity organization. Growth and external fragmentation remain drawbacks.

Evidence in the complete study note

Contiguous allocation uses consecutive blocks; logical block i is start+i and supports direct access, but growth and external fragmentation are problems. Extents allow several contiguous runs. Linked allocation chains blocks and supports growth without reserving a complete contiguous file; locating block i requires traversing preceding links. FAT stores chains in a table; caching that table does not change the chain into a direct arithmetic mapping.OS38

C · incorrect — A separate block-pointer index describes indexed allocation, not contiguous placement itself.

D · incorrect — Physical memory frames are paging units rather than the disk-block contiguity rule for a file.

Question 225

What problem does Linked Allocation solve compared to Contiguous Allocation?

  • A. It speeds up random access
  • B. It eliminates external fragmentation and pre-known file size requirements since blocks can be scattered and linked anywhere
  • C. It reduces internal fragmentation
  • D. It requires less memory for storing file metadata

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

OS29 · Internal and external fragmentation

Correct answer: B — Linked blocks permit growth without reserving a full contiguous run.

OS38 says linked allocation supports growth without reserving a complete contiguous file. Since successive blocks can occupy separate free locations, it removes the external-contiguity problem and the need to reserve the final length beforehand. B captures that advantage. Random access still requires traversing earlier links, internal last-block waste may remain, and no universal smaller-metadata claim follows.

Evidence in the complete study note

Contiguous allocation uses consecutive blocks; logical block i is start+i and supports direct access, but growth and external fragmentation are problems. Extents allow several contiguous runs. Linked allocation chains blocks and supports growth without reserving a complete contiguous file; locating block i requires traversing preceding links. FAT stores chains in a table; caching that table does not change the chain into a direct arithmetic mapping.OS38
Internal fragmentation is unused space inside an allocated unit. External fragmentation is sufficient total free space split into holes that cannot satisfy a required contiguous allocation.OS29

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Locating a later linked block requires traversal, so faster random access is not its general advantage.

B · correct — Separate linked blocks allow incremental growth without a single reserved contiguous region or preknown final length.

C · incorrect — A partially used final block can still waste space internally; linking does not specifically remove that waste.

Question 226

In Indexed Allocation, what is the Index Block?

  • A. A pointer stored in each data block to the next block
  • B. A dedicated block containing all the disk-block addresses (pointers to data blocks) for a specific file
  • C. A table stored at the beginning of the disk for all files
  • D. A block that stores file metadata like size and timestamps

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 43

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace how logical file blocks reach physical blocks and account for pointer metadata.

Study-guide sections

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Correct answer: B — A per-file block of data-block pointers in the simple indexed model.

OS38 defines indexed allocation through a per-file array/tree of block pointers and gives an index-block capacity example. B describes the simple single-index-block form. Large files may need multiple index levels, so all addresses in one block is model-dependent rather than universal. A is linked allocation, C a global-table description, and D ordinary metadata without the defining data-block pointers.

Evidence in the complete study note

Indexed allocation uses a per-file array/tree of block pointers and supports direct lookup with metadata overhead. A Unix inode can combine direct pointers with indirect levels. Check pointer size, block size, index capacity and whether metadata reads are cached.OS38
A 1024-byte index block with 4-byte pointers holds 256 block addresses. With 1024-byte data blocks, one such index level addresses 256 KiB of file data, excluding metadata and any reserved entries.OS38
FAT stores chains in a table; caching that table does not change the chain into a direct arithmetic mapping.OS38

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — A next pointer in each data block describes linked allocation rather than a separate per-file index.

B · correct — In the simple indexed model, a dedicated per-file index contains pointers to the file’s data blocks.

C · incorrect — A global table for all files is not the per-file index block described by indexed allocation.

D · incorrect — Size/timestamps alone do not provide the data-block address index that defines this allocation structure.

Question 227

What is the difference between Sequential Access and Direct Access for files?

  • A. They are identical access methods
  • B. Sequential access reads/writes records in order; direct access allows jumping to any block/record position by specifying its block number
  • C. Direct access is only possible for text files
  • D. Sequential access requires more disk space

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 44

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Trace how logical file blocks reach physical blocks and account for pointer metadata.

Study-guide sections

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS38 · Contiguous, linked and indexed file allocation

Correct answer: B — Sequential access proceeds in order; direct access selects a position.

OS38 contrasts linked traversal of preceding blocks with contiguous start+i and indexed direct lookup. These illustrate the access distinction in B: sequential progression versus selecting a numbered position without reading every preceding record. Nothing restricts direct access to text or requires sequential files to occupy more disk space merely because of their access method.

Evidence in the complete study note

Contiguous allocation uses consecutive blocks; logical block i is start+i and supports direct access, but growth and external fragmentation are problems. Extents allow several contiguous runs. Linked allocation chains blocks and supports growth without reserving a complete contiguous file; locating block i requires traversing preceding links. FAT stores chains in a table; caching that table does not change the chain into a direct arithmetic mapping.OS38
Indexed allocation uses a per-file array/tree of block pointers and supports direct lookup with metadata overhead.OS38

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Order-dependent traversal and direct selection are different access behaviors, not identical methods.

B · correct — Sequential access advances through records; direct access identifies a chosen block/position for access.

C · incorrect — The direct block-lookup rule does not impose a text-file-only restriction.

D · incorrect — Access order alone does not determine a greater storage-space requirement for sequential files.

Question 228

What is a Tree-Structured Directory?

  • A. A directory where all files are in one flat list
  • B. A hierarchical directory organization with a root directory from which subdirectories and files branch out, each file having a unique path
  • C. A directory shared among multiple users
  • D. A temporary directory deleted on reboot

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 45

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Follow namespace structure and distinguish names, underlying files and mounted filesystems.

Study-guide sections

OS37 · Directories, mounting and links

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS37 · Directories, mounting and links

Correct answer: B — A rooted hierarchy of nested directories and files.

OS37 says a tree allows nested directories and absolute/relative paths. In the pure tree model, each entry has a path from the root, matching B. Flat lists instead describe single-level organization; sharing among users and deletion at reboot do not define tree structure. Links can add graph relationships, but that is an extension beyond the pure tree premise.

Evidence in the complete study note

A single-level directory requires unique names in one namespace. Two-level directories separate users. A tree allows nested directories and absolute/relative paths; links can create shared graphs. Modern general-purpose systems commonly present hierarchical namespaces, but a feature name alone does not prove that every system excludes simpler arrangements.OS37

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — One flat file list is single-level organization rather than nested branches from a root.

B · correct — A root with nested subdirectories and paths is the tree hierarchy described here.

C · incorrect — Multiple users sharing a directory does not by itself define its structural hierarchy.

D · incorrect — Deletion on reboot is a lifetime policy rather than the rooted directory-tree organization.

Question 229

What is the difference between Hard Links and Symbolic Links in file systems?

  • A. Hard links can span different file systems; symbolic links cannot
  • B. Hard links create multiple directory entries pointing to the same file's data blocks; symbolic links are special files containing the path to another file
  • C. Symbolic links cannot be deleted
  • D. Hard links are only supported in Windows

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 46

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Follow namespace structure and distinguish names, underlying files and mounted filesystems.

Study-guide sections

OS37 · Directories, mounting and links

OS-S06 · Filesystem links in Linux

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS37 · Directories, mounting and links

OS-S06 · Filesystem links in Linux

Correct answer: B — Hard links name one file object; symbolic links store a pathname.

A hard link supplies another name for the same underlying file object; a symbolic link stores a pathname. Symbolic links can cross filesystems and may dangle, whereas ordinary hard links stay within a filesystem. Linux’s link operation supplies a direct counterexample to the Windows-only claim. unlink can remove a symbolic-link name, so the link is not undeletable. B states the correct distinction.

Evidence in the complete study note

A hard link is another name for the same underlying file object, usually within one filesystem; a symbolic link stores a pathname and can point across filesystems or become dangling. Removing one hard link does not erase content while other names/open references retain it.OS37
Linux provides link() to create another hard-link name for an existing file. The old and new names identify the same file, so hard links are not a Windows-only facility. unlink() removes a filesystem name; when that name is a symbolic link, it removes the link itself. Removing a name and erasing the target’s contents are different operations.OS-S06

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — The boundary is reversed: symbolic links may cross filesystems, while ordinary hard links remain within one.

B · correct — Both hard-link names identify the same underlying file; a symbolic link contains a pathname to resolve.

C · incorrect — A symbolic-link name can be removed with unlink; removing it does not erase the target’s contents.

D · incorrect — Linux provides hard links through link(), so they are not supported only in Windows.

Question 230

What is File System Mounting?

  • A. Creating a new file system
  • B. The process of attaching a file system to a specific location in the existing directory tree, making it accessible
  • C. Deleting old files to free disk space
  • D. Formatting a disk partition

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 47

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Follow namespace structure and distinguish names, underlying files and mounted filesystems.

Study-guide sections

OS37 · Directories, mounting and links

OS35 · Buffering, caching, spooling and clocks

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS37 · Directories, mounting and links

OS35 · Buffering, caching, spooling and clocks

Correct answer: B — Attach a filesystem at a mount point.

OS37 explicitly defines mounting as attaching a filesystem at a mount point and distinguishes it from formatting. B states that integration into the existing namespace. Creation/formatting establish a filesystem’s on-device structure; deleting files frees content space. None of those is the namespace attachment operation requested by mounting.

Evidence in the complete study note

NFS provides remote filesystem access; FTP transfers files rather than itself mounting a local filesystem.OS37
Disk initialization/filesystem creation is OS/tool work, firmware begins boot, and drive firmware may remap bad sectors.OS35

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Creating a new filesystem establishes storage structure rather than attaching it to an existing namespace.

B · correct — Mounting makes a filesystem accessible at a chosen point in the directory hierarchy.

C · incorrect — Deleting files changes contents/free space rather than attaching a filesystem to the namespace.

D · incorrect — Formatting is explicitly distinguished from mounting in the supplied note.

Question 231

In the context of deadlock recovery, what is 'Rollback'?

  • A. Restarting the entire operating system
  • B. Returning a preempted process to a safe previous state after resources are taken back from it during deadlock resolution
  • C. Deleting all processes involved in a deadlock
  • D. Rebooting the computer to free all resources

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 48

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Separate handling before allocation from actions taken after detection, and consider the cost of checking.

Study-guide sections

OS26 · Detection, recovery and intentional ignoring

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS26 · Detection, recovery and intentional ignoring

Correct answer: B — Restore an affected process to a prior checkpoint after reclaiming resources.

OS26 describes reclaiming safely preemptable resources and rolling affected work back to a checkpoint. B states that restoration to a safe prior state after resources are withdrawn. Restarting the entire OS/computer and killing all involved processes are different coarse recovery actions, not the meaning of rollback. Safe checkpoints/restart semantics are required to make withdrawal and restoration coherent.

Evidence in the complete study note

Detection asks whether the current state is deadlocked. Recovery can terminate selected processes or reclaim safely preemptable resources and roll affected work back to a checkpoint. Termination and rollback are both possible recovery tools; choose according to the resource and process conditions.OS26

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Restarting the whole OS is not the checkpoint restoration of affected work that defines rollback.

B · correct — Rollback restores an affected process to a previous recoverable state after safe resource reclamation.

C · incorrect — Terminating processes is a separate recovery tool rather than restoring their earlier execution state.

D · incorrect — Rebooting frees broad machine state but is not the process-checkpoint restoration definition of rollback.

Question 232

What is the purpose of relocation/base and limit registers in the standard contiguous single-process-region memory model? The limit denotes the permitted region length.

  • A. They speed up CPU scheduling decisions
  • B. The relocation register stores the process’s physical base and the limit its permitted length; hardware checks the logical offset before adding the base
  • C. They track file system locations on disk
  • D. They manage virtual memory page tables

Adapted from

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 49

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Apply the stated hole-selection or base-and-bound rule with its explicit convention.

Study-guide sections

OS28 · Contiguous allocation and fit policies

OS30 · Paging, page tables and TLB

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS28 · Contiguous allocation and fit policies

OS30 · Paging, page tables and TLB

Correct answer: B — The relocation register stores the process’s physical base and the limit its permitted length; hardware checks the logical offset before adding the base

In the standard base/limit model, the base identifies the process’s physical starting address and the limit gives its permitted length. Hardware checks that the logical offset is nonnegative and smaller than the limit, then computes base plus offset. This protects accesses to the assigned region. The registers do not describe the OS base and size, schedule CPU work, track disk files or manage page tables.

Evidence in the complete study note

A contiguous allocation gives a process one continuous region. In the standard base/limit model, base is that process’s physical starting address and limit is its permitted length. Check 0≤logical_offset<limit, then physical=base+offset. The relocation register does not normally hold the OS base.OS28
Pages and physical frames have equal fixed size P. Divide virtual address v into page=floor(v/P) and offset=v mod P. The page table maps a page to a frame and protection/status flags.OS30

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — These registers check memory accesses rather than schedule CPU work.

B · correct — The option states the process base, length bound and checked translation.

C · incorrect — Disk-block locations are not the purpose of RAM base/limit registers.

D · incorrect — Simple contiguous base/limit translation does not manage page-table mappings.

Question 233

In Dynamic Linking, what is a key advantage over Static Linking?

  • A. Programs run faster with dynamic linking
  • B. Multiple programs share one copy of library code in memory rather than each program embedding its own copy, saving memory
  • C. Dynamic linking eliminates the need for a page table
  • D. Dynamically linked programs cannot have bugs

Source

  • Operating Systems review questions · Question 50

Hint / Module Reference (Where to Read)

Track when an address or library reference is resolved and the direction of translation.

Study-guide sections

OS27 · Address binding, dynamic loading and linking

OS30 · Paging, page tables and TLB

Answer & Detailed Explanation

Study-guide references

OS27 · Address binding, dynamic loading and linking

OS30 · Paging, page tables and TLB

Correct answer: B — Share suitable library code pages across programs.

OS27 says dynamic linking resolves external-library references at load/runtime and allows suitable library code pages to be shared, whereas static linking copies selected code into the executable. B captures the potential memory saving. It is not an unconditional speed guarantee, does not remove address translation/page tables and cannot eliminate programming bugs. Only shareable code/pages are implied, not arbitrary writable library state.

Evidence in the complete study note

Dynamic linking resolves references to external libraries at load/runtime, allowing suitable library code pages to be shared; static linking copies selected code into the executable.OS27
Pages and physical frames have equal fixed size P. Divide virtual address v into page=floor(v/P) and offset=v mod P. The page table maps a page to a frame and protection/status flags.OS30

Choice by choice

A · incorrect — Dynamic resolution/sharing does not guarantee faster execution for every program or workload.

B · correct — Several processes can share suitable library code pages rather than each embedding a separate static copy.

C · incorrect — Library linking does not remove virtual-address translation or its page-table requirements.

D · incorrect — Choosing dynamic rather than static linking cannot guarantee the absence of programming errors.
